\displaystyle=V\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}((SH)V^{-1}\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert V(SH)^{\dagger})V^{-1},

(178)

where
H
is the Hadamard gate and
S
is the phase gate.
The first two equalities come from the condition for
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
(using the condition on
\rho_{x_{\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert}}
would only yield the first equality).
The latter two equalities come from the conditions for
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y_{U}}
.

We now review some basic facts about Pauli channels.
Recall that for two Pauli channels
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
,
\left\lVert\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\diamond}=|p_{I}-p_{I}^{\prime}|+|p_{X}-p_{X}^{\prime}|+|p_{Y}-p_{Y}^{\prime}|+|p_{Z}-p_{Z}^{\prime}|
and

\lambda_{X}=1-2p_{Y}-2p_{Z},\lambda_{Y}=1-2p_{X}-2p_{Z},\lambda_{Z}=1-2p_{X}-2p_{Y}
.
Therefore, the assumption
\left\lVert\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert_{\diamond}<\epsilon\leq 1/2
implies

\lambda_{X},\lambda_{Y},\lambda_{Z}\geq 1/2.

(179)

The above inequality is useful for bounding the eigenvalues
\lambda_{X},\lambda_{Y},\lambda_{Z}
away from zero.
Using the basic properties of Pauli channels, we can rewrite Eq. (
175
) to Eq. (
178
) as follows.

\displaystyle\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert

\displaystyle=V\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert V^{-1},

(180)

\displaystyle\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert

\displaystyle=V\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert V^{-1},

(181)

\displaystyle(I+\lambda^{\mathcal{P}}_{X}X)

\displaystyle=V(I+\lambda^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}_{X}X)V^{-1},

(182)

\displaystyle(I+\lambda^{\mathcal{Q}}_{Y}Y)

\displaystyle=V(I+\lambda^{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}_{Y}Y)V^{-1},

(183)

where we utilize the fact that
\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert=(I+Z)/2
.
Hence, we have

Z=VZV^{-1},\lambda X=\lambda^{\prime}VXV^{-1},\Lambda Y=\Lambda^{\prime}VYV^{-1},

(184)

where
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime},\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\geq 1/2
from Eq. (
179
).
Because a unitary can be expressed as
V=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi}\exp(\mathrm{i}(aX+bY+cZ))
and an anti-unitary can be expressed as
V=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi}\exp(\mathrm{i}(aX+bY+cZ))K
for
a,b,c,\phi\in\mathbb{R}
and
K
the complex conjugation, Eq. (
184
) implies that
V=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi}I
, which is a contradiction.
Therefore, no distinct world models in
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
are equivalent.

The fact that
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
is not redundant is useful for the following logical reasoning.
Suppose no algorithm can learn the gate-dependent Pauli noise channels to arbitrarily small error.
It means that there exists two distinct world models (with different gate-dependent Pauli noise) in
\mathcal{Q}
such that all experiments have the same output distribution (i.e., the two world models are weakly indistinguishable).
From the non-redundancy of
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
, these two distinct world models are not equivalent to one another.
Hence from Proposition
E.2
, the model class
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
is unlearnable.

On the other hand, suppose there is an algorithm that can learn the gate-dependent Pauli noise channels to arbitrarily small error.
We can build on the Pauli channel learning algorithm to learn
\rho_{x_{\sigma}}
for any state
\sigma
using the following equation

\displaystyle\rho_{x_{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=\frac{I}{2}+\Tr(X\rho_{x_{\sigma}})\frac{X}{2}+\Tr(Y\rho_{x_{\sigma}})\frac{Y}{2}+\Tr(Z\rho_{x_{\sigma}})\frac{Z}{2}

(185)

\displaystyle=\frac{I}{2}+\frac{\Tr(Z\mathcal{E}_{y_{H}}(\rho_{x_{\sigma}}))}{\lambda_{Z}^{H}}\frac{X}{2}-\frac{\Tr(Z\mathcal{E}_{y_{H}}(\mathcal{E}_{y_{S}}(\rho_{x_{\sigma}})))}{\lambda_{Z}^{H}\lambda_{X}^{S}}\frac{Y}{2}+\Tr(Z\rho_{x_{\sigma}})\frac{Z}{2},

(186)

and
\Tr(Z\rho)=\bra{0}\rho\ket{0}-\bra{1}\rho\ket{1}
.
Because every action in
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
can be learned to arbitrarily high accuracy, the model class
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
is learnable.
Together, Theorem
F.1
is equivalent to stating that
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
is unlearnable. In the following, we will prove that
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
is unlearnable.

F.3
Unlearnability of a simpler model class

We combine the basic results proven in Appendix
E
to establish the unlearnability of
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}

The model class
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
is quite complicated.
So we will begin by proving that a simpler model class
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}
is unlearnable. We will then use a set of tools developed in Appendix
E
to show the unlearnability of
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
from the unlearnability of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}
.
The simpler model class
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}
is over a simpler action space
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}=\{0\},\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}=\{h,s,t\},\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}=\{0\}
and the same outcome space
\mathcal{B}=\{0,1\}
.
Here, the three actions
h,s,t
represent Hadamard gate, Phase gate, and
T
gate.
Each world model
\mathcal{W}=\left(\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}},\{\mathcal{E}_{y}\}_{y\in\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}},\{\mathcal{M}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\right)
in
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}

is fully specified by the following conditions,

\displaystyle\rho_{0}=\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,

(187)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{h}(\rho)=\mathcal{P}_{h}(H\rho H^{\dagger}),\,\left\lVert\mathcal{P}_{h}-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert_{\diamond}\leq\epsilon,

(188)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{s}(\rho)=\mathcal{P}_{s}(S\rho S^{\dagger}),\,\left\lVert\mathcal{P}_{s}-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert_{\diamond}\leq\epsilon,

(189)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{t}(\rho)=\mathcal{P}_{t}(T\rho T^{\dagger}),\,\left\lVert\mathcal{P}_{t}-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert_{\diamond}\leq\epsilon,

(190)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}=\{\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert\},

(191)

where
\mathcal{P}_{h},\mathcal{P}_{s},\mathcal{P}_{t}
are Pauli channels.
The simpler model class
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}
considers perfect zero state preparation, perfect computational basis measurement, and noisy Hadamard, phase, and
T
gates that are subject to Pauli noise channels.
And every world model in the simpler model class
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}
is fully determines by the three Pauli noise channels
\mathcal{P}_{h},\mathcal{P}_{s},\mathcal{P}_{t}
.
In the following lemma, we state the unlearnability of the simpler model class
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}
.

Lemma F.2

(Gate-dependent Pauli noise is unlearnable with Hadamard+S+T gates and
\ket{0}
)
.

Given
\epsilon>0
. Consider a qubit system. Suppose we can prepare a perfect zero state
\ket{0}
, measure in the computational basis perfectly, and apply Hadamard gate, phase gate, and
T
gate, where each gate is followed by an unknown gate-dependent Pauli noise channel that is
\epsilon
-close to the identity channel.
It is impossible for any algorithm to learn the gate-dependent Pauli noise channels to arbitrarily small error. Equivalently,
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}
is unlearnable.

The proof of Lemma
F.2
is based on Proposition
E.2
.
The proposition states that when two world models in a model class are weakly indistinguishable and are not equivalent, then the model class is unlearnable.
We will identify two such world models in
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}
, then apply Proposition
E.2
to conclude the proof.
On a high level, the existence of two weakly indistinguishable world models arises from the fact that the geometric structure for the action of Hadamard gate, phase gate, and
T
gate are well-aligned with Pauli noise channels, causing some noise to be indistinguishable from another.

F.4
Actions of Clifford+T on Pauli operators

We begin by illustrating the geometric structure in any quantum experiments one could perform.
Every experiment under the given action spaces
\tilde{\mathcal{X}},\tilde{\mathcal{Y}},\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}
is
x=0,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in\{h,s,t\},z=0
.
Now, we consider the actions of
\mathcal{E}_{h}
,
\mathcal{E}_{s}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{t}
on Pauli operators,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{h}(X)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{h}_{Z}Z,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{s}(X)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{s}_{Y}Y,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{t}(X)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda^{t}_{X}X+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda^{t}_{Y}Y,

(192)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{h}(Y)

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{h}_{Y}Y,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{s}(Y)

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{s}_{X}X,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{t}(Y)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda^{t}_{X}X+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda^{t}_{Y}Y,

(193)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{h}(Z)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{h}_{X}X,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{s}(Z)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{s}_{Z}Z,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{t}(Z)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{t}_{Z}Z,

(194)

where
\lambda^{h}_{X},\lambda^{h}_{Y},\lambda^{h}_{Z}
are the Pauli eigenvalues that define the Pauli noise channel for the Hadamard gate,
\lambda^{s}_{X},\lambda^{s}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{Z}
defines the Pauli noise channel for the phase gate, and
\lambda^{t}_{X},\lambda^{t}_{Y},\lambda^{t}_{Z}
defines the Pauli noise channel for the
T
gate.
Every world model in
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
is specified by the nine real values
\lambda^{h}_{X},\lambda^{h}_{Y},\lambda^{h}_{Z},\lambda^{s}_{X},\lambda^{s}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{Z},\lambda^{t}_{X},\lambda^{t}_{Y},\lambda^{t}_{Z}
.

For an experiment specified by
x=0,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in\{h,t\},z=0
, the probability that the experimental outcome is
0
can be written as

\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\Tr\left(Z(\mathcal{E}_{y_{L}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{E}_{y_{1}})(Z)\right).

(195)

This follows from the identities
\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert=(I+Z)/2,\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert=(I-Z)/2
.
The probability that we obtain
1
as the experimental outcome is equal to one minus the probability for obtaining
0
,

\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\Tr\left(Z(\mathcal{E}_{y_{L}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{E}_{y_{1}})(Z)\right).

(196)

The actions of
\mathcal{E}_{h}
,
\mathcal{E}_{s}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{t}
on Pauli operators are now useful for understanding the term
\Tr\left(Z(\mathcal{E}_{y_{L}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{E}_{y_{1}})(Z)\right)
.

F.4.a
Experiments as multiple particles traversing a graph

We represent the action of the three unitaries
H,S,T
on the three Pauli operators
X,Y,Z
as a small graph with three nodes corresponding to
X,Y,Z
.
We can consider each experiment as particles traversing the graph.
At the start of the experiment, a single particle resides on the node
Z
with an initial value of
1
.
Applying
\mathcal{E}_{h}
corresponds to moving the particle from
Z\rightarrow X,Y\rightarrow Y,X\rightarrow Z
, and the value of the particle will be multiplied by
\lambda_{X}^{h},-\lambda_{Y}^{h},\lambda_{Z}^{h}
accordingly.
Applying
\mathcal{E}_{s}
corresponds to moving the particle from
Y\rightarrow X,X\rightarrow Y,Z\rightarrow Z
, and the value of the particle will be multiplied by
-\lambda_{X}^{s},\lambda_{Y}^{s},\lambda_{Z}^{s}
accordingly.
Applying
\mathcal{E}_{t}
corresponds to a more complicated action.
If the particle resides on
X
, then the particle will
split into two particles: one on
X
and one on
Y
.
The value of the duplicated particle on
X
and
Y
will be equal to the value of the original particle multiplied by
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda^{t}_{X}
and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda^{t}_{Y}
accordingly.
Similarly, if the particle resides on
Y
, the particle will
split into two particles on
X
and
Y
.
The value of the duplicated particle on
X
and
Y
will be equal to the value of the original particle multiplied by
-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda^{t}_{X}
and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda^{t}_{Y}
accordingly.
If the particle resides on
Z
, then the particle will stay at
Z
, and the value of the particle will be multiplied by
\lambda_{Z}^{t}
.
After many applications of the CPTP maps
\mathcal{E}_{y}
, there will be many particles moving on the three-node graph. The number of particles is exponential in the number of
T
gates applied.
At the end of the experiment, we sum up the values of particles residing at
Z
to obtain
\Tr\left(Z(\mathcal{E}_{y_{L}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{E}_{y_{1}})(Z)\right)
.

F.4.b
Polynomial forms and unlearnability

By induction, we can show that the value of each particle residing at
X,Y,Z
can be written as

\displaystyle\lambda^{h}_{X}(\lambda^{h}_{Z}\lambda^{h}_{X})^{k_{X}}

\displaystyle\cdot f_{X}(\lambda^{h}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{X},\lambda^{s}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{Z},\lambda^{t}_{X},\lambda^{t}_{Y},\lambda^{t}_{Z}),

(197)

\displaystyle\lambda^{h}_{X}(\lambda^{h}_{Z}\lambda^{h}_{X})^{k_{Y}}

\displaystyle\cdot f_{Y}(\lambda^{h}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{X},\lambda^{s}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{Z},\lambda^{t}_{X},\lambda^{t}_{Y},\lambda^{t}_{Z}),

(198)

\displaystyle(\lambda^{h}_{Z}\lambda^{h}_{X})^{k_{Z}}

\displaystyle\cdot f_{Z}(\lambda^{h}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{X},\lambda^{s}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{Z},\lambda^{t}_{X},\lambda^{t}_{Y},\lambda^{t}_{Z}),

(199)

accordingly, where
k_{X},k_{Y},k_{Z}
are non-negative integers,
f_{X},f_{Y},f_{Z}
are monomials.
We perform induction according to the dynamics of the particles from the start of the experiment to finish.
In the base case, there are only one particle residing at
Z
with a value of
1
, hence the claimed statement holds.
For each induction step, the traversal/duplication rules guarantee that the above form of values is preserved.
At the end of the experiment, when we sum up the values of particles residing at
Z
, we have the following form for the
Z
expectation value,

\displaystyle\Tr\left(Z(\mathcal{E}_{y_{L}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{E}_{y_{1}})(Z)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(\lambda^{h}_{Z}\lambda^{h}_{X})^{k}f_{k}(\lambda^{h}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{X},\lambda^{s}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{Z},\lambda^{t}_{X},\lambda^{t}_{Y},\lambda^{t}_{Z})

(200)

\displaystyle=p(\lambda^{h}_{Z}\lambda^{h}_{X},\lambda^{h}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{X},\lambda^{s}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{Z},\lambda^{t}_{X},\lambda^{t}_{Y},\lambda^{t}_{Z}),

(201)

where
k
sums over non-negative integers,
f_{k}
is a monomial,
p
is a polynomial.
Together, we can see that the probability for every experimental outcome is a polynomial function in
\lambda^{h}_{Z}\lambda^{h}_{X},\lambda^{h}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{X},\lambda^{s}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{Z},\lambda^{t}_{X},\lambda^{t}_{Y},\lambda^{t}_{Z}
.
Hence, when two world models have the same
\lambda^{h}_{Z}\lambda^{h}_{X}
and the other
\lambda
’s are also equal, the two world models are weakly indistinguishable.

The key point is that a particle departs from
Z
only by moving to
X
through the action of the Hadamard gate, which multiplies its value by
\lambda^{h}_{X}
, and a particle arrives at
Z
only by moving from
X
through the action of a Hadamard gate, which multiplies its value by
\lambda^{h}_{Z}
. Therefore the value acquired by any particle that is initialized at
Z
and returns to
Z
just before the measurement must have the same number of factors of
\lambda^{h}_{Z}
as factors of
\lambda^{h}_{X}
. This same argument still applies if we replace the
T
gate by any gate that is diagonal in the
Z
basis, or it we add to the world model additional gates that are diagonal in the
Z
basis.

From this observation it follows that there exist two distinct world models in the models class
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}
which are weakly indistinguishable.
Recall some basic properties of Pauli channels
sacchi2005optimal
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\rho)

\displaystyle=p_{I}\rho+p_{X}X\rho X+p_{Y}Y\rho Y+p_{Z}Z\rho Z,

(202)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\Tr(\rho)I+\lambda_{X}\Tr(X\rho)X+\lambda_{Y}\Tr(Y\rho)Y+\lambda_{Z}\Tr(Z\rho)Z\right),

(203)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\diamond}

\displaystyle=|p_{I}-p_{I}^{\prime}|+|p_{X}-p_{X}^{\prime}|+|p_{Y}-p_{Y}^{\prime}|+|p_{Z}-p_{Z}^{\prime}|,

(204)

where
(p_{I},p_{X},p_{Y},p_{Z})
is a probability distribution,
\lambda_{X}=1-2p_{Y}-2p_{Z},\lambda_{Y}=1-2p_{X}-2p_{Z},\lambda_{Z}=1-2p_{X}-2p_{Y}
, and
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
are two Pauli channels.
Each world model in
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}
is specified by
\lambda^{h}_{X},\lambda^{h}_{Y},\lambda^{h}_{Z},\lambda^{s}_{X},\lambda^{s}_{Y},\lambda^{s}_{Z},\lambda^{t}_{X},\lambda^{t}_{Y},\lambda^{t}_{Z}
, or equivalently
p^{h}_{X},p^{h}_{Y},p^{h}_{Z},p^{s}_{X},p^{s}_{Y},p^{s}_{Z},p^{t}_{X},p^{t}_{Y},p^{t}_{Z}
as
p_{I}=1-p_{X}-p_{Y}-p_{Z}
.
We consider two world models
\mathcal{W}^{A},\mathcal{W}^{B}
in
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}
to be defined by

\displaystyle p^{h,A}_{X}

\displaystyle=0,\,\,p^{h,A}_{Z}=\epsilon,\,\,p^{h,A}_{Y}=p^{s,A}_{X}=p^{s,A}_{Y}=p^{s,A}_{Z}=p^{t,A}_{X}=p^{t,A}_{Y}=p^{t,A}_{Z}=0,

(205)

\displaystyle p^{h,B}_{X}

\displaystyle=\epsilon,\,\,p^{h,B}_{Z}=0,\,\,p^{h,B}_{Y}=p^{s,B}_{X}=p^{s,B}_{Y}=p^{s,B}_{Z}=p^{t,B}_{X}=p^{t,B}_{Y}=p^{t,B}_{Z}=0.

(206)

The two world models correspond to having
Z
-error or having
X
-error after Hadamard gate. Using the basic properties, it is not hard to check that both
\mathcal{W}^{A},\mathcal{W}^{B}
belong to
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}
.
Furthermore,
\lambda^{h,A}_{X}\lambda^{h,A}_{Z}=\lambda^{h,B}_{X}\lambda^{h,B}_{Z},
and all other
\lambda
’s are equal.
Because the two world models are distinct, using a similar proof as in Appendix
F.2
, we can show that
\mathcal{W}^{A}\not\equiv\mathcal{W}^{B}
, i.e., no unitary or anti-unitary transformation exists that relates
\mathcal{W}^{A}
and
\mathcal{W}^{B}
.
Hence,
\mathcal{W}^{A},\mathcal{W}^{B}
are two world models that are weakly indistinguishable but are not equivalent.
Using Proposition
E.2
, we conclude that
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}
is unlearnable.

F.5
Unlearnability of simple model class implies unlearnability of target model class

We are now ready to prove Theorem
F.1
, i.e., the model class
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
is unlearnable.
The basic structure of the proof is the following. We first utilize the equivalence of learnability after adding some actions, stated in Proposition
E.3
,
E.4
, and
E.6
, to show that a model class
\mathcal{R}^{\epsilon}
, which is a subset of
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
, is unlearnable.
Then, we can use the monotonicity of unlearnability, stated in Proposition
E.1
, to show that
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
is unlearnable because
\mathcal{R}^{\epsilon}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
is unlearnable.

F.5.a
Composing all Clifford gates

We begin with the equivalence of learnability after adding composite CPTP maps as stated in Proposition
E.4
.
We will compose the two actions
h,s\in\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}=\{h,s,t\}
, which corresponds to Hadamard and phase gate subject to gate-dependent Pauli noise; see
\mathcal{E}_{h}
and
\mathcal{E}_{s}
in Equation (
188
) and (
189
).
Because Hadamard and phase gates form a universal gate set for the Clifford group, we can construct any Clifford unitary
C
from a composition of Hadamard gate
H
and phase gate
S
. Let
L^{*}
be the required sequence length to generate every element in the Clifford unitary group.
For any Clifford unitary
C
, we know that
f_{C}(P)=CPC^{\dagger}
is equal to a Pauli operator (up to a phase of
\pm 1
) for any Pauli operator
P\in\{X,Y,Z\}
. Furthermore, up to the phase,
f_{C}
is a permutation function over
\{X,Y,Z\}
.
Hence, for any Clifford unitary
C
, Pauli channel
\mathcal{P}
, and quantum state
\rho
,

C\mathcal{P}(\rho)C^{\dagger}=\mathcal{Q}(C\rho C^{\dagger}),

(207)

where
\mathcal{Q}
is a different Pauli channel satisfying
\left\lVert\mathcal{Q}-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert_{\diamond}=\left\lVert\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert_{\diamond}
.

Now, we compose new CPTP maps based on the model class
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon^{*}}
, where
\epsilon^{*}
will be chosen to be small enough later. The consideration of
\epsilon^{*}
is needed to ensure that all physical operations have a small enough error.
Consider a world model
\mathcal{W}=\left(\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}},\{\mathcal{E}_{y}\}_{y\in\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}},\{\mathcal{M}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\right)
in
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon^{*}}
.
For every Clifford unitary
C
, there exists
y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in\{h,s\}
with
L\leq L^{*}
, such that the composition of the Hadamard and phase gate generates the Clifford unitary
C
.
From the commutation relation between Pauli channel and Clifford unitary in Equation (
207
) we see that

(\mathcal{E}_{y_{L}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{E}_{y_{1}})(\rho)=(\mathcal{Q}_{L}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{Q}_{1})(C\rho C^{\dagger}),

(208)

where
\mathcal{Q}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Q}_{L}
are Pauli channels, and
\left\lVert\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert\leq\epsilon^{*},\forall\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
.
It is not hard to check that the composition of Pauli channel is still a Pauli channel, hence
(\mathcal{Q}_{L}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{Q}_{1})
is a Pauli channel.
Using a telescoping sum and the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{Q}_{L}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{Q}_{1}-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert_{\diamond}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert(\mathcal{Q}_{L}-\mathcal{I})\circ\mathcal{Q}_{L-1}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{Q}_{1}\right\rVert_{\diamond}+\left\lVert(\mathcal{Q}_{L-1}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{Q}_{1})-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert_{\diamond}

(209)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{Q}_{L}-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert_{\diamond}+\left\lVert(\mathcal{Q}_{L-1}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{Q}_{1})-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert_{\diamond}

(210)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{*}+\left\lVert(\mathcal{Q}_{L-1}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{Q}_{1})-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert_{\diamond}

(211)

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon^{*}+\left\lVert(\mathcal{Q}_{L-2}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{Q}_{1})-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert_{\diamond}\leq\ldots\leq L\epsilon^{*}\leq L^{*}\epsilon^{*}.

(212)

We now add in a new action for each element in the Clifford group, excluding the Hadamard and phase gate
H,S
.
After adding these new actions, our action space for CPTP maps has expanded from
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}=\{h,s,t\}
to
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{C}\cup\{T\}
, a union of the Clifford group and the
T
gate
\{T\}
.
We call the new model class with these additional CPTP maps
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\epsilon^{*}}
.
From Proposition
E.4
, we know that adding these new actions do not affect the learnability.
Hence
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\epsilon^{*}}
is unlearnable.
To recap,
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\epsilon^{*}}
is a model class over
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}=\{0\},\mathcal{Y}=\{y_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{C}\cup\{T\}},\mathcal{Z}=\{0\},\mathcal{B}=\{0,1\}
, where
\mathcal{C}
is the Clifford group. Every world model
\mathcal{W}=\left(\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}},\{\mathcal{E}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{Y}},\{\mathcal{M}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\right)
in
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\epsilon^{*}}
satisfies the following condition,

\displaystyle\rho_{0}

\displaystyle=\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,

(213)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{y_{U}}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{U}(U\rho U^{\dagger}),\,\left\lVert\mathcal{P}_{U}-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert_{\diamond}\leq L^{*}\epsilon^{*},

\displaystyle\,\,\forall U\in\mathcal{C}\cup\{T\},

(214)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert\},

(215)

where
\mathcal{P}_{U},\forall U\in\mathcal{C}\cup\{T\}
are Pauli channels.
However,
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\epsilon^{*}}
does not contain all the world models that satisfy the above conditions.

F.5.b
Composing all quantum states

The next step in the proof is to use the equivalence of learnability after adding composite initial states and adding mixtures of initial states as in Proposition
E.3
and
E.6
.
We compose the zero initial state
\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert
with the Clifford+T gates to generate all pure states up to some small errors.
Then, we can add mixtures of the pure states to generate all quantum states up to some small errors.
We consider
L^{s}
to be the minimum integer such that for all pure states
\ket{\psi}
, there exists a sequence
U_{1},\ldots,U_{\ell}
consisting of Clifford gates and
T
gates with
\ell\leq L^{s}
and

\left\lVert\lvert\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi\rvert-(U_{\ell}\ldots U_{1})\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert(U_{1}\ldots U_{\ell})^{\dagger}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\epsilon/2.

(216)

From the Solovay-Kitaev theorem
dawson2005solovay
and follow-up works
harrow2002efficient
;
selinger2012efficient
,
L^{s}=\mathcal{O}(\log(1/\epsilon))
.
From Proposition
E.3
, the model class with the additional states is constructed by specifying the set of additional actions
\Xi
, a constant
L
, and a function
f
with
f(\xi)=(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})
.
Here, we consider
\Xi=\{\ket{\psi}\}_{\ket{\psi}:\mathrm{pure\,state}}
to be the space of all pure states,
L=L^{s}
, and we define
f
to map a pure state
\ket{\psi}
to
(0,y_{U_{1}},\ldots,y_{U_{\ell}})
, where
U_{1},\ldots,U_{\ell}
are the unitaries for approximating the pure state
\ket{\psi}
according to Eq. (
216
).
For every world model
\mathcal{W}=\left(\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}},\{\mathcal{E}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{Y}},\{\mathcal{M}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\right)
in
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\epsilon^{*}}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\lvert\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi\rvert-(\mathcal{E}_{y_{U_{\ell}}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{E}_{y_{U_{1}}})(\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert)\right\rVert_{1}\leq\left\lVert\lvert\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi\rvert-(U_{\ell}\ldots U_{1})\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert(U_{1}\ldots U_{\ell})^{\dagger}\right\rVert_{1}

(217)

\displaystyle+\left\lVert(\mathcal{E}_{y_{U_{\ell}}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{E}_{y_{U_{1}}})(\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert)-(U_{\ell}\ldots U_{1})\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert(U_{1}\ldots U_{\ell})^{\dagger}\right\rVert_{1}

(218)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon/2+\ell L^{*}\epsilon^{*}\leq\epsilon/2+L^{s}L^{*}\epsilon^{*},

(219)

where the second-to-last inequality uses Eq. (
216
), a telescoping sum, and the triangle inequality, and the last inequality uses
\ell\leq L^{s}
.
We have now added actions associated to generating arbitrary pure states.
Proposition
E.3
shows that adding these pure states will maintain the unlearnability.
Now, for all mixed states, we only need to make use of the fact that any single-qubit mixed state
\rho
can be written as
\rho=p\lvert\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi\rvert+(1-p)\lvert\phi\rangle\!\langle\phi\rvert
for some
0<p<1
and two orthogonal pure states
\ket{\psi},\ket{\phi}
.
Suppose
y_{U_{1}},\ldots,y_{U_{\ell}}
specifies the unitaries to approximate
\ket{\psi}
and
y_{U_{1}}^{\prime},\ldots,y_{U_{\ell^{\prime}}}^{\prime}
specifies the unitaries to approximate
\ket{\phi}
. Then by triangle inequality and Eq. (
219
),

\left\lVert\rho-\left[p(\mathcal{E}_{y_{U_{\ell}}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{E}_{y_{U_{1}}})(\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert)+(1-p)(\mathcal{E}_{y_{U_{\ell^{\prime}}}^{\prime}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{E}_{y_{U_{1}}^{\prime}})(\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert)\right]\right\rVert_{1}\leq\epsilon/2+L^{s}L^{*}\epsilon^{*}.

(220)

Using Proposition
E.6
, we can add all the mixed states without altering the unlearnability.
We have now create a model class
\mathcal{R}^{\epsilon^{*}}
over
\mathcal{X}=\{x_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma:\mathrm{state}},\mathcal{Y}=\{y_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{C}\cup\{T\}},\mathcal{Z}=\{0\},\mathcal{B}=\{0,1\}
, where
\mathcal{C}
is the Clifford group. Every world model
\mathcal{W}=\left(\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}},\{\mathcal{E}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{Y}},\{\mathcal{M}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\right)
in
\mathcal{R}^{\epsilon^{*}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho_{x_{\sigma}}-\sigma\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon/2+L^{s}L^{*}\epsilon^{*},

\displaystyle\,\,\forall\sigma:\mathrm{state},

(221)

\displaystyle\rho_{x_{\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert}}

\displaystyle=\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,

(222)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{y_{U}}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{U}(U\rho U^{\dagger}),\,\left\lVert\mathcal{P}_{U}-\mathcal{I}\right\rVert_{\diamond}\leq L^{*}\epsilon^{*},

\displaystyle\,\,\forall U\in\mathcal{C}\cup\{T\},

(223)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert\}.

(224)

Furthermore, from the fact that
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\epsilon^{*}}
is unlearnable, and Proposition
E.3
and
E.6
, we have
\mathcal{R}^{\epsilon^{*}}
is unlearnable.
Now, if we choose
\epsilon^{*}=\epsilon/(2L^{s}L^{*})
, then every world model in
\mathcal{R}^{\epsilon^{*}}
is in
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
.
Hence, using monotonicity of learnability in Proposition
E.1
, we have
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
is unlearnable. This concludes the proof of Theorem
F.1
by recalling the equivalence of Theorem
F.1
and the unlearnability of
\mathcal{Q}^{\epsilon}
.

Remark 2
.

By tracing through the proof, one can see that the unavoidable noise floor for learning the gate-dependent Pauli noise channel is
\epsilon^{*}
, which is of order
\epsilon/\log(1/\epsilon)
.

APPENDIX G
Noise and unlearnability

We now give two examples of unlearnable model classes.
Consider
d
-dimensional world models.
We focus on the action spaces
\mathcal{X}=\{\sigma\}_{\sigma:\mathrm{state}},\mathcal{Y}=\{U\}_{U\in\mathrm{SU}(d)},\mathcal{Z}=\{0\}
.
Consider
\epsilon>0
and the model class
\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}
over
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z}
that consists of two world models
\mathcal{W}^{A}=\left(\{\rho^{A}_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}},\{\mathcal{E}^{A}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{Y}},\{\mathcal{M}^{A}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\right)
, where

\displaystyle\rho^{A}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=(1-\epsilon)\sigma+\epsilon\frac{I}{d},

\displaystyle\forall\sigma:\mathrm{state},

(225)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}_{U}(\rho)

\displaystyle=U\rho U^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\forall U\in\mathrm{SU}(d),

(226)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{A}_{0}

\displaystyle=\left\{\lvert b\rangle\!\langle b\rvert\right\}_{b=1,\ldots,d},

(227)

and
\mathcal{W}^{B}=\left(\{\rho^{B}_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}},\{\mathcal{E}^{B}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{Y}},\{\mathcal{M}^{B}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\right)
, where

\displaystyle\rho^{B}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\sigma,

\displaystyle\forall\sigma:\mathrm{state},

(228)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{B}_{U}(\rho)

\displaystyle=U\rho U^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\forall U\in\mathrm{SU}(d),

(229)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{B}_{0}

\displaystyle=\left\{(1-\epsilon)\lvert b\rangle\!\langle b\rvert+\epsilon\frac{I}{d}\right\}_{b=1,\ldots,d},

(230)

Verbally, the model class
\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}
contains world models where we have perfect unitaries, but the initial state or the computational basis measurement is subject to a depolarization noise of strength
\epsilon
.

We also consider another model class
\mathcal{S}^{\Omega}
that encompasses world models where states, unitaries, and the computational basis measurements are all noisy.
Formally,
\mathcal{S}^{\Omega}
is over the same set of action spaces
\mathcal{X}=\{\sigma\}_{\sigma:\mathrm{state}},\mathcal{Y}=\{U\}_{U\in\mathrm{SU}(d)},\mathcal{Z}=\{0\}
.
And
\mathcal{S}^{\Omega}
contains all world models
\mathcal{W}=\left(\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}},\{\mathcal{E}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{Y}},\{\mathcal{M}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\right)
, where
\rho_{x}
is a quantum state,
\mathcal{E}_{y}
is a CPTP map, and
\mathcal{M}_{z}
is a POVM.
It is not hard to see that
\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\Omega}
. Hence, the two world models
\mathcal{W}^{A},\mathcal{W}^{B}
are also in
\mathcal{S}^{\Omega}
.

We will now provide a formal proof showing that
\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}
is unlearnable.
For any experiments
E=(\sigma,U_{1},\ldots,U_{\ell},0)
, where
\sigma\in\mathcal{X},U_{1},\ldots,U_{\ell}\in\mathcal{Y},0\in\mathcal{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\Tr\left(M^{A}_{0b}(\mathcal{E}^{A}_{U_{\ell}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{E}^{A}_{U_{1}})(\rho^{A}_{\sigma})\right)

\displaystyle=(1-\epsilon)\bra{b}U_{\ell}\ldots U_{1}\sigma U_{1}^{\dagger}\ldots U_{\ell}^{\dagger}\ket{b}+\frac{\epsilon}{d},\,\,\forall b\in\{1,\ldots,d\},

(231)

\displaystyle\Tr\left(M^{B}_{0b}(\mathcal{E}^{B}_{U_{\ell}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{E}^{B}_{U_{1}})(\rho^{B}_{\sigma})\right)

\displaystyle=(1-\epsilon)\bra{b}U_{\ell}\ldots U_{1}\sigma U_{1}^{\dagger}\ldots U_{\ell}^{\dagger}\ket{b}+\frac{\epsilon}{d},\,\,\forall b\in\{1,\ldots,d\}.

(232)

Hence the two world models are weakly indistinguishable.
It is also easy to check that the two world models are not equivalent to one another, hence they describe different physical realities.
By Theorem
A.8
, if the two world models are equivalent, then there exists a unitary or anti-unitary transformation
U
such that
\rho^{B}_{\sigma}=U\rho^{A}_{\sigma}U^{-1}
for any quantum state
\sigma
.
We can use this relation to deduce that
\Tr((\rho^{B}_{\sigma})^{2})=\Tr((\rho^{A}_{\sigma})^{2})
, i.e., the purity of the two states
\rho^{A}_{\sigma},\rho^{B}_{\sigma}
must be equal, for all state
\sigma
.
However, for
\epsilon>0
and pure state
\sigma=\lvert\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi\rvert
, the purity of
\rho^{A}_{\sigma}
is less than one, but the purity of
\rho^{B}_{\sigma}
is one.
Hence, the two world models
\mathcal{W}^{A},\mathcal{W}^{B}
are not equivalent.

Because
\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}
contains two weakly indistinguishable world models that are not equivalent to one another,
\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}
is unlearnable according to Proposition
E.2
.
Then using Proposition
E.1
, the monotonicity of unlearnability,
\mathcal{S}^{\Omega}
is unlearnable because
\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\Omega}
.

APPENDIX H
Learning under gate-independent noise on Clifford gates

We give the detailed proof for Theorem
5
in this appendix.

H.1
Review on unitary design for Clifford gates

We recall the following well-known facts which relate Clifford gates to unitary designs.
These two properties will be used through to design our learning algorithm and to prove its correctness.
Furthermore, we will be using a substantial amount of tensor network manipulation, where only the final simplified results are shown.
Readers unfamiliar with tensor network manipulations could refer to reviews in
bridgeman2017hand
;
chen2021exponential
.

Lemma H.1

(Unitary design for Clifford gates
dankert2009tdesign
;
webb2015clifford
;
zhu2016clifford
)
.

Consider
n>0
to be the number qubits. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the set of all Clifford gates over
n
qubits and let
d=2^{n}
. We have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}CAC^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\frac{I}{d}\Tr(A),

\displaystyle(\mbox{1-design})

(233)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}C^{\otimes 2}B(C^{\dagger})^{\otimes 2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}-1}\left(I\Tr(B)+S\Tr(SB)-\frac{1}{d}S\Tr(B)-\frac{1}{d}I\Tr(SB)\right),

\displaystyle(\mbox{2-design})

(234)

where
A
is an
2^{n}\times 2^{n}
complex matrix,
B
is a complex tensor living in the tensor product space of two
2^{n}\times 2^{n}
complex matrices,
S
is the swap operator over the two tensor product components.

H.2
Learning noisy zero state, Clifford gate noise, and noisy basis measurement

Two sets of randomized experiments are conducted to learn about the noisy initial state
\rho_{0}\approx\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert
, the Clifford gate noise
{{\mathcal{N}}}\approx{\mathcal{I}}
, and the noisy computational basis measurement
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}=\{M_{b}\}_{b\in\{0,1\}^{n}}
with
M_{b}\approx\lvert b\rangle\!\langle b\rvert
.
The first set of
N_{A}
experiments prepares
\rho_{0}
, evolves by
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{C}
for a random Clifford
C
, and measures
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
.
The second set of
N_{B}
experiments prepares
\rho_{0}
, evolves by
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{C_{1}}
for a random Clifford
C_{1}
, evolves by
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{C_{2}}
for a second random Clifford
C_{2}
, and measures
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
.
We denote the two sets of experimental outcomes as

\displaystyle\left(C^{(A,i)},b^{(A,i)}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\right),

\displaystyle\quad\forall i=1,\ldots,N_{A},

(235)

\displaystyle\left(C^{(B,i)}_{1},C^{(B,i)}_{2},b^{(B,i)}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\right),

\displaystyle\quad\forall i=1,\ldots,N_{B}.

(236)

We will also define the POVM
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\{M^{\prime}_{b}={{\mathcal{N}}}^{\dagger}(M_{b})\}_{b\in\{0,1\}^{n}}
which is equivalent to applying the Clifford gate noise
{{\mathcal{N}}}
followed by performing
the noisy computational basis measurement
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
.

In the following, we will denote
d=2^{n}
,
f=\bra{0^{n}}\rho_{0}\ket{0^{n}}
,
g=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\bra{b}M^{\prime}_{b}\ket{b}
,
\mathbb{I}[A]
to be the indicator function, i.e.,
\mathbb{I}[A]=1
if
A
is true and
\mathbb{I}[A]=0
if
A
is false.
Because
f
is unlearnable, we can set
f
to whatever value we want.
One practical choice is to set
f=1
since
\rho_{0}\approx\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert
.
Under the assumption that
\rho_{0}\approx\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert
and
M^{\prime}_{b}={{\mathcal{N}}}^{\dagger}(M_{b})\approx M_{b}\approx\lvert b\rangle\!\langle b\rvert
, we can consider

f=\bra{0^{n}}\rho_{0}\ket{0^{n}}\in[0.9,1],\quad\bra{b}M^{\prime}_{b}\ket{b}\in[0.9,1.1],\quad g=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\bra{b}M^{\prime}_{b}\ket{b}\in[0.9,1.1].

(237)

This follows from the fact that the noise is bounded.

H.2.a
Learning noisy basis measurement conflated with Clifford gate noise

We now construct various estimators that characterize the POVM
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\{M^{\prime}_{b}\}_{b\in\{0,1\}^{n}}
. We start with the simplest estimator,

\widehat{\Tr(M^{\prime}_{b})}=\frac{1}{N_{A}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{A}}d\,{\mathbb{I}}\left[b^{(A,i)}=b\right].

(238)

Using the unitary
1
-design property of Clifford gates, we have

\E\left[\widehat{\Tr(M^{\prime}_{b})}\right]=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}d\Tr\left(M^{\prime}_{b}C\rho_{0}C^{\dagger}\right)=\Tr(M^{\prime}_{b}).

(239)

Hence, we can estimate
\Tr(M^{\prime}_{b})
to arbitrarily small error with large enough
N_{A}
.
The next estimator is slightly more complicated and uses
\widehat{\Tr(M^{\prime}_{b})}
,

\widehat{M^{\prime}_{b}}=\frac{d^{2}-1}{f-\frac{1}{d}}\left[\frac{1}{N_{A}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{A}}{\mathbb{I}}\left[b^{(A,i)}=b\right]C^{(A,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C^{(A,i)})^{\dagger}\right]-\frac{1-\frac{f}{d}}{{f-\frac{1}{d}}}\widehat{\Tr(M^{\prime}_{b})}I.

(240)

Using the unitary
2
-design property of Clifford gates, we have

\E\left[\widehat{M^{\prime}_{b}}\right]=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\frac{d^{2}-1}{f-\frac{1}{d}}\Tr(M^{\prime}_{b}C\rho_{0}C^{\dagger})C\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert C^{\dagger}-\frac{1-\frac{f}{d}}{{f-\frac{1}{d}}}\Tr(M^{\prime}_{b})I=M^{\prime}_{b}.

(241)

Hence, we can estimate the POVM element
M^{\prime}_{b}
to arbitrarily small error with large enough
N_{A}
.

We will also utilize the following estimator to estimate
g=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\bra{b}M^{\prime}_{b}\ket{b}
.

\widehat{g}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\bra{b}\widehat{M^{\prime}_{b}}\ket{b}=\frac{d^{2}-1}{df-1}\left[\frac{1}{N_{A}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{A}}\bra{b^{(A,i)}}C^{(A,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C^{(A,i)})^{\dagger}\ket{b^{(A,i)}}\right]-\frac{d-f}{{df-1}}.

(242)

The second equality follows from
\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\widehat{\Tr(M^{\prime}_{b})}=d
.
From the equality in Eq. (
241
) and linearity of expectation, we have

\E\left[\widehat{g}\right]=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\bra{b}M^{\prime}_{b}\ket{b}=g.

(243)

Hence, we can estimate the scalar value
g
to arbitrarily small error with large enough
N_{A}
.

H.2.b
Learning noisy zero state

If
g
is perfectly known, then the estimator for
\rho_{0}
is given as follows.

\widehat{\rho_{0}}^{(g*)}=\frac{d^{2}-1}{dg-1}\left[\frac{1}{N_{A}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{A}}(C^{(A,i)})^{\dagger}\lvert b^{(A,i)}\rangle\!\langle b^{(A,i)}\rvert C^{(A,i)}\right]-\frac{d-\frac{g}{d}}{dg-1}I.

(244)

Notice that in the noiseless setting (
g=1
), the above estimator is exactly equal to the classical shadow representation based on randomized Clifford measurements
huang2020predicting
;
huang2022learning
.
Using the unitary
2
-design property of Clifford gates, we have

\E\left[\widehat{\rho_{0}}^{(g*)}\right]=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\frac{d^{2}-1}{dg-1}\Tr(M^{\prime}_{b}C\rho_{0}C^{\dagger})C^{\dagger}\lvert b\rangle\!\langle b\rvert C-\frac{d-\frac{g}{d}}{dg-1}I=\rho_{0}.

(245)

Hence, we can estimate
\rho_{0}
to arbitrarily small error with large enough
N_{A}
when
g
is perfectly known.
However, since
g
is estimated using
\hat{g}
, we will use the following estimator instead.

\widehat{\rho_{0}}=\frac{d^{2}-1}{d\hat{g}-1}\left[\frac{1}{N_{A}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{A}}(C^{(A,i)})^{\dagger}\lvert b^{(A,i)}\rangle\!\langle b^{(A,i)}\rvert C^{(A,i)}\right]-\frac{d-\frac{\hat{g}}{d}}{d\hat{g}-1}I.

(246)

We use that fact that, with large enough
N_{A}
,
\hat{g}
can be made arbitrarily close to
g
.
Because
g\in[0.9,1.1]
from Eq. (
237
), with large enough
N_{A}
,
\frac{d^{2}-1}{d\hat{g}-1}
and
\frac{d-\frac{\hat{g}}{d}}{d\hat{g}-1}
can be made arbitrarily close to
\frac{d^{2}-1}{dg-1}
and
\frac{d-\frac{g}{d}}{dg-1}
, respectively.
Hence, with Eq. (
245
), the estimator
\widehat{\rho_{0}}
can be made arbitrarily close to
\rho_{0}
with large enough
N_{A}
.

H.2.c
Learning Clifford gate noise

So far, we have only used the first set of experiments.
We are now ready to learn the Clifford gate noise
{{\mathcal{N}}}
using the second set of experiments.
We will use the Choi matrix representation of a quantum channel.
Recall that the Choi matrix of a channel
{{\mathcal{N}}}
is given by

\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}\equiv({{\mathcal{N}}}\otimes{\mathcal{I}})(\lvert\omega\rangle\!\langle\omega\rvert),

(247)

where
\ket{\omega}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\ket{b}\otimes\ket{b}
is the maximally entangled state over
2n
qubits, and
{\mathcal{I}}
is the identity channel on
n
qubits.
We first estimate the state
{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)
.
The basic idea is to intentionally neglect
C_{1}^{(B,i)}
in the data.
Then
\rho_{0}
evolved under an unknown random Clifford gate
C_{1}
followed by the gate noise
{{\mathcal{N}}}
is equal to the state
{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)
from the unitary
1
-design property of random Clifford gate.
Then we can essentially use the same estimator as Eq. (
244
) to learn the state
{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)
.
Assuming
g
is known, then we can use the estimator,

\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)}^{(g*)}=\frac{d^{2}-1}{dg-1}\left[\frac{1}{N_{B}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{B}}(C_{2}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}\lvert b^{(B,i)}\rangle\!\langle b^{(B,i)}\rvert C_{2}^{(B,i)}\right]-\frac{d-\frac{g}{d}}{dg-1}I.

(248)

From unitary
1
-design and
2
-design property, we have

\displaystyle\E\left[\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)}^{(g*)}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}|^{2}}\sum_{C_{1}\in\mathcal{C}}\sum_{C_{2}\in\mathcal{C}}\frac{d^{2}-1}{dg-1}\Tr(M^{\prime}_{b}C_{2}{{\mathcal{N}}}(C_{1}\rho_{0}C_{1}^{\dagger})C_{2}^{\dagger})C_{2}^{\dagger}\lvert b\rangle\!\langle b\rvert C_{2}-\frac{d-\frac{g}{d}}{dg-1}I

(249)

\displaystyle={{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d).

(250)

Similar to learning
\rho_{0}
, we only have an estimate for
g
given by
\hat{g}
.
Hence, the estimator we will use is

\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)}=\frac{d^{2}-1}{d\hat{g}-1}\left[\frac{1}{N_{B}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{B}}(C_{2}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}\lvert b^{(B,i)}\rangle\!\langle b^{(B,i)}\rvert C_{2}^{(B,i)}\right]-\frac{d-\frac{\hat{g}}{d}}{d\hat{g}-1}I.

(251)

With large enough
N_{A}
,
\hat{g}
can be made arbitrarily close to
g
.
So, from Eq (
250
), the estimator
\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)}
can be made arbitrarily close to
{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)
with large enough
N_{A}
and
N_{B}
.

We now present the estimator for the Choi matrix of
{{\mathcal{N}}}
.
We will begin by assuming that
g
is perfectly known, then we will approximate
g
by
\widehat{g}
.
The estimator
\widehat{\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{(g*)}
is defined as

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{(g*)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(d^{2}-1)^{2}}{(df-1)(dg-1)}\left[\frac{1}{N_{B}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{B}}\left((C_{2}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}\lvert b^{(B,i)}\rangle\!\langle b^{(B,i)}\rvert C_{2}^{(B,i)}\right)\otimes\left(C_{1}^{(B,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C_{1}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}\right)^{T}\right]

(252)

\displaystyle-\frac{(d-\frac{1}{d})(d-\hat{g})}{(df-1)(dg-1)}\,\,\left[I\otimes I\right]-\frac{1-\frac{f}{d}}{f-\frac{1}{d}}\,\,\left[\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)}^{(g*)}\otimes I\right].

(253)

Using unitary
2
-design property of Clifford gates and Eq. (
250
), we have

\displaystyle\E\left[\widehat{\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{(g*)}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}|^{2}}\sum_{C_{1}\in\mathcal{C}}\sum_{C_{2}\in\mathcal{C}}\frac{(d^{2}-1)^{2}}{(df-1)(g-1)}\Tr(M^{\prime}_{b}C_{2}{{\mathcal{N}}}(C_{1}\rho_{0}C_{1}^{\dagger})C_{2}^{\dagger})

(254)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\left(C_{2}^{\dagger}\lvert b\rangle\!\langle b\rvert C_{2}\right)\otimes\left(C_{1}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert C_{1}^{\dagger}\right)^{T}

(255)

\displaystyle-\frac{(d-\frac{1}{d})(1-\frac{g}{d^{2}})}{(df-1)(g-1)}I\otimes I-\frac{1-\frac{f}{d}}{f-\frac{1}{d}}{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)\otimes I

(256)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}.

(257)

Similar to before, we only have an estimate for
g
given by
\hat{g}
, so we will instead use the following estimator, i.e., replacing all
g
by
\hat{g}
and
\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)}^{(g*)}
by
\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)}
.

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(d^{2}-1)^{2}}{(df-1)(d\hat{g}-1)}\left[\frac{1}{N_{B}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{B}}\left((C_{2}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}\lvert b^{(B,i)}\rangle\!\langle b^{(B,i)}\rvert C_{2}^{(B,i)}\right)\otimes\left(C_{1}^{(B,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C_{1}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}\right)^{T}\right]

(258)

\displaystyle-\frac{(d-\frac{1}{d})(d-\hat{g})}{(df-1)(d\hat{g}-1)}\,\,\left[I\otimes I\right]-\frac{1-\frac{f}{d}}{f-\frac{1}{d}}\,\,\left[\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)}\otimes I\right].

(259)

With large enough
N_{A},N_{B}
,
\hat{g}
and
\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)}
can be made arbitrarily close to
g
and
{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)
.
So, from Eq (
257
), the estimator
\widehat{\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}}
can be made arbitrarily close to
\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}
with large enough
N_{A}
and
N_{B}
.
With the estimator for Choi matrix
\widehat{\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}}
, we can obtain the estimator
\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}}
for the original CPTP map
{{\mathcal{N}}}
by applying the linear invertible transformation between Choi matrix and CPTP map.

H.2.d
Learning noisy basis measurement

After learning
\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}
through the estimator
\widehat{\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}}
, we can obtain the noisy computational basis measurement
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}=\{M_{b}\}_{b\in\{0,1\}^{n}}
by considering the following estimator.

\widehat{M_{b}}=\left(\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{\dagger}\right)^{-1}\widehat{M^{\prime}_{b}},\quad\forall b\in\{0,1\}^{n}.

(260)

Note that
\widehat{M^{\prime}_{b}}
can be made arbitrarily close to
M^{\prime}_{b}={{\mathcal{N}}}^{\dagger}(M_{b})
and
\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}}
can be made arbitrarily close to
{{\mathcal{N}}}
.
Because
{{\mathcal{N}}}
is assumed to be close to the identity, the difference between
\left(\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{\dagger}\right)^{-1}
and
\left({{\mathcal{N}}}^{\dagger}\right)^{-1}
can be made arbitrarily small by increasing
N_{A},N_{B}
.
Hence,
\widehat{M_{b}}
can be arbitrarily close to
M_{b}
with large enough
N_{A},N_{B}
.

H.3
Learning any state, CPTP map, and POVM

After learning
\rho_{0},{{\mathcal{N}}},{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
, we present the learning of any physical operation in the system.
The procedures are very similar to the previous subsection.

H.3.a
Learning POVM

Given any POVM
{\mathcal{M}}_{z}=\{M_{zb}\}_{b\in{\mathcal{B}}}
, where
{\mathcal{B}}
is a set denoting all the possible outcomes.
We can learn
M_{zb}
by conducting the following randomized experiments for
N_{z}
times: prepare
\rho_{0}
, evolve under
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{C}
for a random Clifford gate
C
, measure
{\mathcal{M}}_{z}
.
We denote the sets of experimental outcomes as

\displaystyle\left(C^{(z,i)},b^{(z,i)}\in{\mathcal{B}}\right),

\displaystyle\quad\forall i=1,\ldots,N_{z}.

(261)

The procedure is very similar to that for learning
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
.
First, we consider the POVM
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{z}=\{M^{\prime}_{zb}={{\mathcal{N}}}^{\dagger}(M_{z}b)\}_{b\in{\mathcal{B}}}
, which conflates
{\mathcal{M}}_{z}
with the Clifford gate noise
{{\mathcal{N}}}
.
We can learn
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{z}
using the following estimators based on the data we obtained.

\displaystyle\widehat{\Tr(M^{\prime}_{zb})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{z}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{z}}d\,{\mathbb{I}}\left[b^{(z,i)}=b\right],

(262)

\displaystyle\widehat{M^{\prime}_{zb}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{2}-1}{f-\frac{1}{d}}\left[\frac{1}{N_{z}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{z}}{\mathbb{I}}\left[b^{(z,i)}=b\right]C^{(z,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C^{(z,i)})^{\dagger}\right]-\frac{1-\frac{f}{d}}{{f-\frac{1}{d}}}\widehat{\Tr(M^{\prime}_{zb})}I.

(263)

Using the same analysis as Appendix
H.2.a
, we can show that
\widehat{M^{\prime}_{zb}}
can be made arbitrarily close to
M^{\prime}_{zb}
with large enough
N_{z}
.
Then similar to Appendix
H.2.d
, we can learn
M_{zb}
using the following estimator after obtaining
\hat{{{\mathcal{N}}}}
from the algorithms presented in Appendix
H.2.c
.

\widehat{M_{zb}}=\left(\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{\dagger}\right)^{-1}\widehat{M^{\prime}_{zb}},\quad\forall b\in{\mathcal{B}}.

(264)

With large enough
N_{A},N_{B},N_{z}
, we can make
\widehat{M_{zb}}
arbitrarily close to
M_{zb}
.

H.3.b
Learning states

Given any state
\rho_{x}
. We can learn
\rho_{x}
by conducting the following randomized experiments
N_{x}
times: prepare
\rho_{x}
, evolve under
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{C}
for a random Clifford gate
C
, measure
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
.
We denote the sets of experimental outcomes as

\displaystyle\left(C^{(x,i)},b^{(x,i)}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\right),

\displaystyle\quad\forall i=1,\ldots,N_{x}.

(265)

We can obtain an estimate for
\rho_{x}
using the following formula.

\widehat{\rho_{x}}=\frac{d^{2}-1}{d\hat{g}-1}\left[\frac{1}{N_{x}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}(C^{(x,i)})^{\dagger}\lvert b^{(x,i)}\rangle\!\langle b^{(x,i)}\rvert C^{(x,i)}\right]-\frac{d-\frac{\hat{g}}{d}}{d\hat{g}-1}I.

(266)

With large enough
N_{A},N_{B},N_{x}
, we can make
\widehat{\rho_{x}}
arbitrarily close to
\rho_{x}
.
The analysis is the same as that in Appendix
H.2.b
.

H.3.c
Learning CPTP maps

Given any CPTP map
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}
. We can learn
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}
by conducting the following randomized experiments for
N_{y}
times: prepare
\rho_{0}
, evolve under
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{C_{1}}
for a random Clifford gate
C_{1}
, evolve under
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}
, evolve under
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{C_{2}}
for a different random Clifford gate
C_{2}
, measure
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
.
We denote the sets of
N_{y}
experimental outcomes as follows.

\displaystyle\left(C_{1}^{(y,i)},C_{2}^{(y,i)},b^{(y,i)}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\right),

\displaystyle\quad\forall i=1,\ldots,N_{y}.

(267)

We first learn the quantum process
{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}_{y}={{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}\circ{{\mathcal{N}}}
, which conflates
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}
with the Clifford gate noise
{{\mathcal{N}}}
. Following the same analysis as Appendix
H.2.c
but replacing
{{\mathcal{N}}}
by
{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}_{y}
, we define the following estimators.

\displaystyle\widehat{{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}_{y}(I/d)}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{2}-1}{d\hat{g}-1}\left[\frac{1}{N_{y}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{y}}(C_{2}^{(u,i)})^{\dagger}\lvert b^{(y,i)}\rangle\!\langle b^{(y,i)}\rvert C_{2}^{(y,i)}\right]-\frac{d-\frac{\hat{g}}{d}}{d\hat{g}-1}I.

(268)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi_{{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}_{y}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(d^{2}-1)^{2}}{(df-1)(d\hat{g}-1)}\left[\frac{1}{N_{y}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{y}}\left((C_{2}^{(y,i)})^{\dagger}\lvert b^{(y,i)}\rangle\!\langle b^{(y,i)}\rvert C_{2}^{(y,i)}\right)\otimes\left(C_{1}^{(y,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C_{1}^{(y,i)})^{\dagger}\right)^{T}\right]

(269)

\displaystyle-\frac{(d-\frac{1}{d})(d-\hat{g})}{(df-1)(d\hat{g}-1)}\,\,\left[I\otimes I\right]-\frac{1-\frac{f}{d}}{f-\frac{1}{d}}\,\,\left[\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)}\otimes I\right].

(270)

With large enough
N_{A},N_{B},N_{y}
, we can make
\widehat{\Phi_{{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}_{y}}}
arbitrarily close to
\Phi_{{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}_{y}}
, the Choi matrix for the CPTP map
{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}_{y}
.
Hence, we can obtain the estimator
\widehat{{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}_{y}}
for
{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}_{y}
by transforming the Choi matrix back to the CPTP map.
Now we simply need to invert the conflation with
{{\mathcal{N}}}
by considering

\widehat{{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}}=\widehat{{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}_{y}}\circ(\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}})^{-1}.

(271)

Because the Clifford gate noise
{{\mathcal{N}}}
is assumed to be close to the identity, the difference between
\left(\widehat{{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{\dagger}\right)^{-1}
and
\left({{\mathcal{N}}}^{\dagger}\right)^{-1}
can be made arbitrarily small by increasing
N_{A},N_{B}
.
Therefore, we can make the difference between
\widehat{{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}}
and
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}
arbitrarily small with large enough
N_{A},N_{B},N_{y}
.

H.4
Sample complexity for learning Clifford gate noise

All the previous analyses could be equipped with rigorous convergence guarantee using concentration inequalities similar to quantum state/process tomography based on randomized measurements
guta2020fast
;
surawy2021projected
.
As an example, we present the sample complexity for learning Clifford gate noise
{{\mathcal{N}}}
with
{{\mathcal{N}}}(I)=I
.
The reconstruction of
{{\mathcal{N}}}
under the condition
{{\mathcal{N}}}(I)=I
was previously studied in
kimmel2014robust
;
roth2018recovering
;
helsen2021estimating
, where learning algorithms based on interleaved randomized benchmarking
magesan2012efficient
have been devised.
We will show that the proposed algorithm is much more efficient than the best known sample complexity of
\mathcal{O}(d^{8})
in
helsen2021estimating
for learning the Choi matrix
\Phi_{{{\mathcal{N}}}}
up to a constant error in Hilbert-Schmidt norm.

Under the assumption that
{{\mathcal{N}}}(I)=I
, we do not need to estimate
{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)
because
{{\mathcal{N}}}(I/d)=I/d
.
Hence, the estimator in Eq. (
259
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(d^{2}-1)^{2}}{(df-1)(d\hat{g}-1)}\left[\frac{1}{N_{B}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{B}}\left((C_{2}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}\lvert b^{(B,i)}\rangle\!\langle b^{(B,i)}\rvert C_{2}^{(B,i)}\right)\otimes\left(C_{1}^{(B,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C_{1}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}\right)^{T}\right]

(272)

\displaystyle-\frac{(d-\frac{1}{d})(d-\hat{g})}{(df-1)(d\hat{g}-1)}\,\,\left[I\otimes I\right]-\frac{1-\frac{f}{d}}{df-1}\,\,\left[I\otimes I\right].

(273)

Furthermore, using the definition of
\hat{g}
in Eq. (
242
), we see that

(df-1)(d\hat{g}-1)=(d^{2}-1)\left(d\left[\frac{1}{N_{A}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{A}}\bra{b^{(A,i)}}C^{(A,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C^{(A,i)})^{\dagger}\ket{b^{(A,i)}}\right]-1\right)

(274)

is independent of
f
. Furthermore, we can simplify the coefficients for
I\otimes I
using

-\frac{(d-\frac{1}{d})(d-\hat{g})}{(df-1)(d\hat{g}-1)}-\frac{1-\frac{f}{d}}{df-1}=-\frac{(d-\frac{1}{d})^{2}}{(df-1)(d\hat{g}-1)}+\frac{1}{d^{2}},

(275)

which is also independent of
f
because
(df-1)(d\hat{g}-1)
is independent of
f
.
We can now rewrite the estimator
\widehat{\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}}
as the following
f
-independent expression,

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}}

\displaystyle=(d^{2}-1)\frac{\frac{1}{N_{B}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{B}}\left((C_{2}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}\lvert b^{(B,i)}\rangle\!\langle b^{(B,i)}\rvert C_{2}^{(B,i)}\right)\otimes\left(C_{1}^{(B,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C_{1}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}\right)^{T}}{d\left[\frac{1}{N_{A}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{A}}\bra{b^{(A,i)}}C^{(A,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C^{(A,i)})^{\dagger}\ket{b^{(A,i)}}\right]-1}

(276)

\displaystyle-(d^{2}-1)\frac{(I/d)\otimes(I/d)}{d\left[\frac{1}{N_{A}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{A}}\bra{b^{(A,i)}}C^{(A,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C^{(A,i)})^{\dagger}\ket{b^{(A,i)}}\right]-1}+(I/d)\otimes(I/d).

(277)

We will now present the algorithm for estimating
\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}
under the Pauli basis.
Consider two
n
-qubit Pauli operators
P,Q\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}
.
We would estimate
\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})
based on the above expression for
\widehat{\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}}
.
The estimation procedure is separated into three cases.

H.4.a
P=I
and
Q=I

This is the simplest case.
Because
\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}
is a quantum state, we have
\Tr(\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})=1
.
Hence, we can obtain a perfect estimate for
\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})
as it is always one.

H.4.b
Exactly one of
P
and
Q
is equal to
I

This is also a simple case. Recall that a non-identity Pauli operator has trace equal to zero. Then, using the definition of Choi matrix
\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}
and
{{\mathcal{N}}}(I)=I
, we have

\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})=0.

(278)

Hence, we can obtain a perfect estimate for
\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})
as it is always zero.

H.4.c
P\neq I
and
Q\neq I

Using the fact that a non-identity Pauli operator has trace equal to zero, we have

\Tr((P\otimes Q)\widehat{\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}})=(d^{2}-1)\frac{\frac{1}{N_{B}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{B}}\bra{b^{(B,i)}}C_{2}^{(B,i)}P(C_{2}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}\ket{b^{(B,i)}}\bra{0^{n}}(C_{1}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}Q^{T}C_{1}^{(B,i)}\ket{0^{n}}}{d\left[\frac{1}{N_{A}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{A}}\bra{b^{(A,i)}}C^{(A,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C^{(A,i)})^{\dagger}\ket{b^{(A,i)}}\right]-1}.

(279)

We will directly use the above formula to estimate
\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})
.

To analyze the error in the above estimator, we separately consider the convergence of the numerator and the denominator.
We begin with the denominator,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Y}}

\displaystyle=d\left[\frac{1}{N_{A}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{A}}\bra{b^{(A,i)}}C^{(A,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C^{(A,i)})^{\dagger}\ket{b^{(A,i)}}\right]-1

(280)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{A}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{A}}\left[d\bra{b^{(A,i)}}C^{(A,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C^{(A,i)})^{\dagger}\ket{b^{(A,i)}}-1\right].

(281)

Lemma H.2

(Concentration for the denominator)
.

Fix
\epsilon>0
.
Given
N_{A}=\mathcal{O}(1/\epsilon^{2})
. With probability at least
0.99
, we have

\left|{\mathcal{Y}}-\frac{(df-1)(dg-1)}{(d-1)(d+1)}\right|<\epsilon,

(282)

where
\frac{(df-1)(dg-1)}{(d-1)(d+1)}\in[0.21,1.21]
from Eq. (
237
).

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}=d\bra{b^{(A,i)}}C^{(A,i)}\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert(C^{(A,i)})^{\dagger}\ket{b^{(A,i)}}-1
.
From unitary
2
-design of random Clifford gate, we have the following identity,

\E[{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}]=\frac{(df-1)(dg-1)}{(d-1)(d+1)}.

(283)

Then, using the unitary
3
-design property of random Clifford gate
webb2015clifford
;
zhu2016clifford
and the conditions in Eq. (
237
), we have

\Var[{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}]\leq\frac{1}{|{\mathcal{C}}|}\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\Tr(M^{\prime}_{b}C\rho_{0}C^{\dagger})d^{2}(\bra{b}C\lvert 0^{n}\rangle\!\langle 0^{n}\rvert C^{\dagger}\ket{b})^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1).

(284)

The claim then follows from
{\mathcal{Y}}=\frac{1}{N_{A}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{A}}{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}
and Chebyshev’s inequality.
∎

Next, we can analyze the numerator,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{B}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{B}}(d^{2}-1)\bra{b^{(B,i)}}C_{2}^{(B,i)}P(C_{2}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}\ket{b^{(B,i)}}\bra{0^{n}}(C_{1}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}Q^{T}C_{1}^{(B,i)}\ket{0^{n}}.

(285)

Lemma H.3

(Concentration for the numerator)
.

Fix
0.5>\epsilon,\delta>0
.
Given
N_{B}=\mathcal{O}(d^{2}\log(1/\delta)/\epsilon^{2})
. With probability at least
1-\delta
,

\left|{\mathcal{X}}-\frac{(df-1)(dg-1)}{(d-1)(d+1)}\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})\right|<\epsilon,

(286)

where
\frac{(df-1)(dg-1)}{(d-1)(d+1)}\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})\in[0.21,1.21]
from Eq. (
237
).

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=(d^{2}-1)\bra{b^{(B,i)}}C_{2}^{(B,i)}P(C_{2}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}\ket{b^{(B,i)}}\bra{0^{n}}(C_{1}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}Q^{T}C_{1}^{(B,i)}\ket{0^{n}}
.
From unitary
2
-design of random Clifford gate and the fact that non-identity Pauli has trace zero, we have,

\E[{\mathcal{X}}_{i}]=\frac{(df-1)(dg-1)}{(d-1)(d+1)}\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}).

(287)

Because
C_{2}^{(B,i)}
is a random Clifford gate,
C_{2}^{(B,i)}P(C_{2}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}
is proportional to a random non-identity Pauli
\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}\setminus\{I^{\otimes n}\}
.
If
C_{2}^{(B,i)}P(C_{2}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}
is not proportional to a Pauli-Z operator
\{I,Z\}^{\otimes n}
, then
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0
.
Similarly
(C_{1}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}Q^{T}C_{1}^{(B,i)}
is proportional to a random non-identity Pauli.
And,
C_{1}^{(B,i)}Q^{T}(C_{1}^{(B,i)})^{\dagger}
is not proportional to a Pauli-Z operator
\{I,Z\}^{\otimes n}
, then
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0
.
Because
C_{1},C_{2}
are independent random gates, we have
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\neq 0
with probability at most

\frac{(d-1)}{(d^{2}-1)}\times\frac{(d-1)}{(d^{2}-1)}=\frac{1}{(d+1)^{2}}.

(288)

Furthermore, we have
|{\mathcal{X}}_{i}|\leq(d^{2}-1)
with probability one.
Therefore, we have

\Var[{\mathcal{X}}_{i}]\leq\E[{\mathcal{X}}_{i}^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{(d+1)^{2}}\times(d^{2}-1)^{2}=(d-1)^{2}\leq d^{2}.

(289)

From Bernstein’s inequality and the definition that
{\mathcal{X}}=\frac{1}{N_{B}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{B}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
, we conclude the proof.
∎

We can now establish the following statement.

Lemma H.4

(Combine numerator and denominator)
.

Given
0<\epsilon<0.1
.
Assume Eq. (
282
) and (
286
) both hold. Then

\left|\frac{{\mathcal{X}}}{{\mathcal{Y}}}-\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})\right|<155\epsilon.

(290)

Proof.

The proof follows from the following analysis.

\displaystyle\left|\frac{{\mathcal{X}}}{{\mathcal{Y}}}-\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{{\mathcal{X}}}{{\mathcal{Y}}}-\frac{{\mathcal{X}}}{\frac{(df-1)(dg-1)}{(d-1)(d+1)}}\right|+\left|\frac{{\mathcal{X}}}{\frac{(df-1)(dg-1)}{(d-1)(d+1)}}-\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})\right|

(291)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1.21+\epsilon\right)\left|\frac{1}{{\mathcal{Y}}}-\frac{1}{\frac{(df-1)(dg-1)}{(d-1)(d+1)}}\right|

(292)

\displaystyle+5\left|{\mathcal{X}}-\frac{(df-1)(dg-1)}{(d-1)(d+1)}\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})\right|

(293)

\displaystyle<1.5\times 100\epsilon+5\epsilon=155\epsilon.

(294)

The first line uses triangle inequality.
The second inequality uses the fact that

\frac{(df-1)(dg-1)}{(d-1)(d+1)},\frac{(df-1)(dg-1)}{(d-1)(d+1)}\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})\in[0.21,1.21].

(295)

The third inequality uses Eq. (
295
), the condition that
0<\epsilon<0.1
, and

\left|\frac{1}{a}-\frac{1}{b}\right|<\frac{1}{\min(a,b)^{2}}\times\left|a-b\right|,\forall a,b>0.

(296)

This concludes the proof.
∎

H.4.d
Final result

Recall the following Pauli basis representation of a
2n
-qubit quantum state,

\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}=\sum_{P,Q\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}}\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})\frac{(P\otimes Q)}{d^{2}}.

(297)

We can learn the coefficients
\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})
using the above strategy.
We denote the estimated coefficients as
\hat{o}(\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}},P,Q)\in\mathbb{R}
.
We can then obtain a reconstruction for
\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}
as

\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{N}}}(N_{A},N_{B})=\sum_{P,Q\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}}\hat{o}(\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}},P,Q)\frac{(P\otimes Q)}{d^{2}}.

(298)

We can now combine the previous results to show the sample complexity to learn the Choi matrix of the Clifford gate noise
\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}
up to
\epsilon
error in Hilbert Schmidt norm.

Theorem H.5

(Sample complexity for learning Clifford gate noise)
.

Given
0<\epsilon<0.1d
. Assume the noise is bounded as stated in Eq. (
237
).
With
N_{A}=\mathcal{O}(d^{2}/\epsilon^{2})
and
N_{B}=\mathcal{O}(d^{4}\log(d)/\epsilon^{2})
,

\left\lVert\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{N}}}(N_{A},N_{B})-\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}<\epsilon,

(299)

with probability at least
0.99
, where
\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}=\Tr(X^{2})
is the Hilbert-Schmidt norm (Frobenius norm).

Proof.

Let
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon/d
.
We can employ union bound and Lemma
H.2
,
H.3
,
H.4
to show that given
N_{A}=\mathcal{O}(1/(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2})=\mathcal{O}(d^{2}/\epsilon^{2})
and
N_{B}=\mathcal{O}(d^{2}\log(d)/(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2})=\mathcal{O}(d^{4}\log(d)/\epsilon^{2})
and
0<\epsilon^{\prime}<0.1
, we have

\left|\hat{o}(\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}},P,Q)-\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})\right|<\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon/d,

(300)

with probability at least
0.99
. Therefore, we have

\left\lVert\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{N}}}(N_{A},N_{B})-\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{P,Q\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}}\left|\hat{o}(\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}},P,Q)-\Tr((P\otimes Q)\Phi_{{\mathcal{N}}})\right|^{2}}<\epsilon

(301)

with probability at least
0.99
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

APPENDIX I
Foundations for predicting extrinsic behavior

Recall that Definition
C.6
considers the predictability of properties in the world model.
Here, we refer to a property as a function that maps a world model to a real value.
Given action spaces
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z}
and an outcome space
\mathcal{B}
.
There is a class of properties that are guaranteed to be predictable: the probability of an outcome
b\in\mathcal{B}
for an experiment specified by the sequence of actions
x\in\mathcal{X},y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}}
.
We refer to such properties as the extrinsic behavior of the world model.
Formally, this class of properties is given in the following definition.

Definition I.1

(Extrinsic behavior of world model)
.

Given action spaces
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z}
and an outcome space
\mathcal{B}
.
We consider extrinsic behavior to be a set of properties
{\mathcal{F}}=\{f:\mathcal{W}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\}
. For any
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
, there is an experiment
E=(x\in\mathcal{X},y_{1}\in\mathcal{Y},\ldots,y_{\ell}\in\mathcal{Y},z\in\mathcal{Z})
, and an outcome
b\in{\mathcal{B}}
, such that

f(\mathcal{W})=\Tr(M_{zb}(\mathcal{E}_{y_{\ell}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{E}_{y_{1}})(\rho_{x})),

(302)

for any world model
{\mathcal{W}}=\left(\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}},\{\mathcal{E}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{Y}},\{\mathcal{M}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\right)
.
We refer to
f
in the set as
f^{\mathrm{(behav)}}_{x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell},z,b}
.

In the following, we give several fundamental results regarding the task of predicting extrinsic behavior. In particular, we will give a rigorous performance guarantee for existing gate-set tomography protocols formulated in terms of learning the extrinsic behavior of a world model.

I.1
Hardness in predicting extrinsic behaviors

The goal of this appendix is to prove the following theorem.

Theorem I.2

(Worst case complexity for predicting extrinsic behaviors; Restatement of Theorem
3
)
.

Consider finite sets
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}
with
|\mathcal{B}|\geq 2
and
|\mathcal{B}|=\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{poly}(|\mathcal{X}|,|\mathcal{Y}|,|\mathcal{Z}|))
.
Given
\epsilon,L>0
.
For any model class
\mathcal{Q}
over
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}
, there is an algorithm that uses

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)

(303)

experiments to accurately predict
f^{\mathrm{(behav)}}_{x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z,b}(\mathcal{W})
up to
\epsilon
-error for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}},b\in{\mathcal{B}}
with high probability.
Furthermore, there exists a model class
\mathcal{Q}
over
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}
such that

\Omega\left(\frac{|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)

(304)

experiments are required to accurately predict
f^{\mathrm{(behav)}}_{x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z,b}(\mathcal{W})
up to
\epsilon
-error for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}},b\in{\mathcal{B}}
with high probability.

The first part of the theorem is straightforward.
We consider the algorithm that runs through all possible experiments with
L
CPTP maps. For each experiment specified by
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}}
, the algorithm performs the experiment a number of

K=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log\left(|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}||{\mathcal{B}}|\right)}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)

(305)

times.
For any world model
\mathcal{W}\in\mathcal{Q}
, using Hoeffding’s inequality, the
K
experimental outcomes can be used to estimate the probability of an outcome
b\in\mathcal{B}
for the experiment
E=(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z)
, i.e.,
f^{\mathrm{(behav)}}_{x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z,b}(\mathcal{W})
, up to an error
\epsilon
with probability
\geq 1-1/(10|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}||{\mathcal{B}}|)
.
Using the union bound, with probability
\geq 0.9
, we can estimate
f^{\mathrm{(behav)}}_{x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z,b}(\mathcal{W})
up to an error
\epsilon
for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}},b\in{\mathcal{B}}
.
The total number of experiments used by the algorithm is

|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|K=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|\log\left(|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}||{\mathcal{B}}|\right)}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|}{\epsilon^{2}}\right),

(306)

because of the assumption that
|\mathcal{B}|=\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{poly}(|\mathcal{X}|,|\mathcal{Y}|,|\mathcal{Z}|))
.

The second part of the theorem is more nontrivial.
For this part of the proof, we will only utilize two elements in
{\mathcal{B}}
, which we denote as
b_{0},b_{1}
.
We begin by constructing a model class
\mathcal{Q}
. The model class
{\mathcal{Q}}
contains a null world model
\mathcal{W}_{0}
given by

\displaystyle\rho_{x}

\displaystyle=\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{X},

(307)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,

\displaystyle\forall y\in\mathcal{Y},

(308)

\displaystyle M_{zb_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}I,

\displaystyle\forall z\in\mathcal{Z},

(309)

\displaystyle M_{zb_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}I,

\displaystyle\forall z\in\mathcal{Z},

(310)

\displaystyle M_{zb}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\forall z\in\mathcal{Z},\forall b\in{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\{b_{0},b_{1}\},

(311)

where
I
is the identity operator.
The model class
\mathcal{Q}
also contains another
2|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|
world models.
Each of the world models is given by
\mathcal{W}_{x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z,s}
where
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}},s=\pm 1
.
Because
x
could be one of
|{\mathcal{X}}|
elements,
y_{\ell}
could be one of
|{\mathcal{Y}}|
elements for any
\ell=1,\ldots,L
,
z
could be one of
|{\mathcal{Z}}|
elements, and
s
could take one of the two values
\pm 1
, we have

\left|\{\mathcal{W}_{x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z,s}\}_{x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}},s\in\{\pm 1\}}\right|=2|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|

(312)

The world model
\mathcal{W}_{x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z,s}
is defined by the following equalities

\displaystyle\rho_{x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,

\displaystyle\forall x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}\setminus\{x\},

(313)

\displaystyle\rho_{x}

\displaystyle=\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert,

(314)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y_{\ell}}(\lvert\ell\rangle\!\langle\ell\rvert)

\displaystyle=\lvert\ell+1\rangle\!\langle\ell+1\rvert,

\displaystyle\forall 1\leq\ell\leq L,

(315)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}(\lvert\ell\rangle\!\langle\ell\rvert)

\displaystyle=\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,

\displaystyle\forall 0\leq\ell\leq L+1,y\in{\mathcal{Y}},\,\mbox{s.t.}\,\forall 1\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq L,y\neq y_{\ell^{\prime}},

(316)

\displaystyle M_{zb_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(I+3s\epsilon\lvert L+1\rangle\!\langle L+1\rvert),

\displaystyle\forall z\in\mathcal{Z},

(317)

\displaystyle M_{zb_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(I-3s\epsilon\lvert L+1\rangle\!\langle L+1\rvert),

\displaystyle\forall z\in\mathcal{Z},

(318)

\displaystyle M_{zb}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\forall z\in\mathcal{Z},\forall b\in{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\{b_{0},b_{1}\},

(319)

For the null world model
{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
, the outcome distribution is always a uniform distribution over
b_{0},b_{1}
for any experiments.
For world model
\mathcal{W}_{x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z,s}
, only for one experiment, i.e., when we consider
E=(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z)
, the outcome distribution is a biased distribution over
b_{0},b_{1}
, in particular, we see
b_{0}
with probability
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3}{2}s\epsilon
and
b_{1}
with probability
\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{2}s\epsilon
. For all other experiments, the outcome distribution is again a uniform distribution over
b_{0},b_{1}
.
We will also denote
\mathcal{W}_{x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z,s}
as
\mathcal{W}_{E,s}
, where
E=(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z)
is an experiment.

We consider the true world model to be
\mathcal{W}_{0}
with probability
1/2
and to be
\mathcal{W}_{x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z,s}
for a particular choice of
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}},s=\pm 1
with probability
1/(4|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|)
.
If there is a learning algorithm that could accurately predict
f^{\mathrm{(behav)}}_{x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z,b}(\mathcal{W})
up to
\epsilon
-error for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}},b\in{\mathcal{B}}
, then the algorithm can be used to check if the true world model is equal to
\mathcal{W}_{0}
.
Recall that for
\mathcal{W}_{0}
, all experiments yield an outcome probability distribution that is uniform over
\{b_{0},b_{1}\}
.
For any world model in
\mathcal{Q}\setminus\{\mathcal{W}_{0}\}
, the probability for only one of the possible experiments specified by
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}}
will be a biased distribution.
Hence, we can determine whether the true world model is equal to
\mathcal{W}_{0}
by checking if for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}}
, the probability to obtain both
b_{0}
and
b_{1}
are close to
1/2
.
This enables us to map the learning problem to a two-hypothesis testing problem.
And the question becomes:

How many experiments are required to test if the true world model is equal to
\mathcal{W}_{0}
?

We will utilize the proof techniques given in
huang2021information
;
chen2021exponential
to answer the above question.

A learning algorithm is represented by a tree where each node represents the collection of data received from all prior experiments.
Each edge is labeled by an experiment
E
and an outcome
b\in\{b_{0},b_{1}\}
from that experiment.
We only consider outcomes
\{b_{0},b_{1}\}
because the other outcomes happen with zero probability by construction.
The probability for traversing that edge is the product of the probability that the experiment
E
is performed at this stage of the learning algorithm and the probability for seeing the outcome
b
for the experiment
E
.
The depth
T
of the tree is the total number of experiments conducted by the learning algorithm.
After conducting
T
experiments, we will arrive at a leaf node of the tree.
The probability to arrive at a leaf node depends on the true world model.

We consider two events: when the true world model is
\mathcal{W}_{0}
, and when the true world model is not equal to
\mathcal{W}_{0}
.
We distinguish the two events based on the data we collected from the
T
experiments, i,e., which leaf node we arrived at.
In order to successfully distinguish between the two events with a constant probability, we need the total variation distance of the probability distribution over the leaf nodes to be of
\Omega(1)
. Formally, this is known as LeCam’s two point method.

Each leaf node
\ell
is specified by the path from root to the leaf node, which is a sequence of
T
experiments and their corresponding outcomes.
Hence, we write each leaf node as
\ell_{(E_{1},\beta_{1})\ldots,(E_{T},\beta_{T})}
, where
\beta_{t}\in\{b_{0},b_{1}\}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
.
For the null world model
\mathcal{W}_{0}
, the probability of a leaf node is given by

p_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\ell_{(E_{1},\beta_{1})\ldots,(E_{T},\beta_{T})})=\prod_{t=1}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{2}p_{(E_{1},\beta_{1})\ldots,(E_{t-1},\beta_{t-1})}(E_{t})\right),

(320)

where
p_{(E_{1},\beta_{1})\ldots,(E_{t-1},\beta_{t-1})}(E_{t})
is the probability that the algorithm would perform the experiment
E_{t}
when the algorithm have run
t-1
experiments
E_{1},\ldots,E_{t-1}
and the corresponding outcomes are
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{t-1}\in\{b_{0},b_{1}\}
.
Recall that we denote the world model
\mathcal{W}_{x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z,s}
defined in Eq. (
313
) to Eq. (
319
) as
\mathcal{W}_{E,s}
, where
E
is a tuple
(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z)
.
Note that an experiment
E_{t}
as defined in Def.
A.2
is also a tuple.
We will define the delta function
\delta_{E,E^{\prime}}
to be one if the two tuples
E,E^{\prime}
are exactly the same, and zero otherwise.
From Eq. (
313
) to Eq. (
319
), the probability of a leaf node for the world model
\mathcal{W}_{E,s}
is given by

p_{\mathcal{W}_{E,s}}(\ell_{(E_{1},\beta_{1})\ldots,(E_{T},\beta_{T})})=\prod_{t=1}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(1+3s\epsilon\delta_{E_{t},E}\sign(\beta_{t})\right)p_{(E_{1},\beta_{1})\ldots,(E_{t-1},\beta_{t-1})}(E_{t})\right),

(321)

where
\sign(\beta_{t})=1
if
\beta_{t}=b_{0}
and
\sign(\beta_{t})=-1
if
\beta_{t}=b_{1}
.

We can now write down the total variation distance between the probability distribution over the leaf nodes under the two events (true world model
=\mathcal{W}_{0}
or true world model
\neq{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
).
In the event that the true world model
\neq{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
, we have the true world model is equal to
\mathcal{W}_{E,s}
for a particular choice of
E=(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z),x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}},s=\pm 1
with probability
1/(2|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|)
.
The total variation distance is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell}\left|p_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\ell)-\E_{E,s}p_{\mathcal{W}_{E,s}}(\ell)\right|=\sum_{\ell:\,\,p_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\ell)\geq\E_{E,s}p_{\mathcal{W}_{E,s}}(\ell)}\left(p_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\ell)-\E_{E,s}p_{\mathcal{W}_{E,s}}(\ell)\right)

(322)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell:\,\,p_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\ell)\geq\E_{E,s}p_{\mathcal{W}_{E,s}}(\ell)}p_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\ell)\eta\leq\eta,

(323)

where the parameter
\eta
satisfies

\frac{\E_{E,s}p_{\mathcal{W}_{E,s}}(\ell)}{p_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\ell)}\geq 1-\eta,\forall\,\mbox{leaf node }\ell.

(324)

The expectation
\E_{E,s}
with
E=(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z)
is a uniform distribution over
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}},s=\pm 1
.
We will now find a parameter
\eta
that satisfies the above condition.
Given
\ell=\ell_{(E_{1},\beta_{1})\ldots,(E_{T},\beta_{T})}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\E_{E,s}p_{\mathcal{W}_{E,s}}(\ell)}{p_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\E_{E,s}\prod_{t=1}^{T}\left(1+3s\epsilon\delta_{E_{t},E}\sign(\beta_{t})\right)

(325)

\displaystyle=\E_{E,s}\exp\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\log\left(1+3s\epsilon\delta_{E_{t},E}\sign(\beta_{t})\right)\right]

(326)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left[\E_{E,s}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\log\left(1+3s\epsilon\delta_{E_{t},E}\sign(\beta_{t})\right)\right]

(327)

\displaystyle=\exp\left[\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\E_{E}\log\left(1-9\epsilon^{2}\delta_{E_{t},E}\right)\right]

(328)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left[\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|}\log\left(1-9\epsilon^{2}\right)\right]

(329)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left[-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|}9\epsilon^{2}\right]

(330)
