\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{9T\epsilon^{2}}{|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|}.

(331)

The third line is Jensen’s inequality.
The fourth line uses the fact that
s=\pm 1
uniformly.
The fifth line uses the fact that
E=(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z)
is distributed uniformly over
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}}
.
The second-to-last line uses
\log(1-x)\geq-2x,\forall x\in[0,0.79]
which is satisfied given
\epsilon<0.29
.
The last line uses
\exp(x)\geq 1+x,\forall x\in\mathbb{R}
.
Together, we can choose
\eta=\frac{9T\epsilon^{2}}{|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|}
and we have established

\Omega(1)\leq\mathrm{TV}\leq\frac{9T\epsilon^{2}}{|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|}.

(332)

We have thus proved that the number of experiments must be at least
T=\Omega(|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}|^{L}|\mathcal{Z}|/\epsilon^{2})
.

APPENDIX J
A general theorem for predicting extrinsic behavior

In order to avoid the worst-case complexity proved in the previous subsection, we need to make a stronger assumption about the true world model.
We present one such assumption that is closely related to the assumption used in existing gate set tomography protocols
greenbaum2015introduction
;
blume2017demonstration
;
nielsen2020gate
;
brieger2021compressive
.
Intuitively, the assumption is that we can efficiently find a complete set of states that span the set of states we can generate using the world model, and a complete set of POVM elements whose span includes the complete set of states.
In the worst case, such as in the world models we constructed in the previous subsection, we cannot find a complete set of states and POVM elements efficiently.
As a result, we saw that the optimal complexity scales very badly (exponentially in
L
).

Before presenting the assumption, we will recall some basic concepts.
Given a world model
\mathcal{W}=\left(\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}},\{\mathcal{E}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{Y}},\{\mathcal{M}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\right)
.
We can compose a state
\rho
and a CPTP map
\mathcal{E}
to prepare a new state
\mathcal{E}(\rho)
.
Similarly, we can compose a POVM
{\mathcal{M}}
and a CPTP map
{{\mathcal{E}}}
to compose a new POVM
{\mathcal{M}}\circ{{\mathcal{E}}}
that is equivalent to first applying
{{\mathcal{E}}}
then measure using
{\mathcal{M}}
.
We refer to the states that could be prepared by composing a finite sequence of CPTP maps
\mathcal{E}_{y}
acting on some initial state
\rho_{x}
as states that could be generated in
{\mathcal{W}}
.

J.1
Assumptions

We assume that we have found a linearly independent set of composed states
\{\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{K_{1}}\}
such that
\mathrm{span}(\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{K_{1}})
contains all states that can be generated in
{\mathcal{W}}
, where
\mathrm{span}(\ldots)
means all linear combinations with real coefficients.
This is equivalent to stating that for any state
\rho
that can be generated in
{\mathcal{W}}
, there exists a unique set of coefficients
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\rho=\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\alpha_{k_{1}}\rho_{k_{1}}.

(333)

Because
\rho
and
\rho_{k_{1}}
for all
k_{1}\in\{1,\ldots,K_{1}\}
are quantum states, we have
\sum_{k_{1}}\alpha_{k_{1}}=1
.
We consider
R_{1}>0
to be a constant such that

\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}|\alpha_{k_{1}}|\leq R_{1}

(334)

for any quantum state
\rho
in
\mathrm{span}(\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{K_{1}})
.

Remark 3
.

R_{1}<\infty
because a finite-dimensional quantum state space is compact.

We also assume that we have found a set of composed POVM elements
\{M_{1},\ldots,M_{K_{2}}\}
such that
\mathrm{span}(\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{K_{1}})\subseteq\mathrm{span}(M_{1},\ldots,M_{K_{2}})
.
This assumption implies that for any
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{1}}
, if

\forall k_{2}\in\{1,\ldots,K_{2}\},\Tr\left(M_{k_{2}}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\alpha_{k_{1}}\rho_{k_{1}}\right)=\Tr\left(M_{k_{2}}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\alpha_{k_{1}}^{\prime}\rho_{k_{1}}\right),

(335)

then
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
.
In particular, we consider
R_{2}>0
to be a constant such that

\left\lVert\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\right\rVert_{1}\leq R_{2}\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{K_{2}}\left|\Tr\left(M_{k_{2}}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\alpha_{k_{1}}\rho_{k_{1}}\right)-\Tr\left(M_{k_{2}}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\alpha_{k_{1}}^{\prime}\rho_{k_{1}}\right)\right|

(336)

for any
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{1}}
.

Remark 4
.

R_{2}<\infty
because the homogeneity of Eq. (
336
) enables maximization to find
R_{2}
over the compact space
\left\lVert\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\right\rVert_{1}=1
.

J.2
Learning a frame

We are now ready to present an efficient learning algorithm that can be used to predict the extrinsic behavior of the world model
\mathcal{W}
.
For each
k_{2}=1,\ldots,K_{2}
, we consider the POVM element
M_{k_{2}}
to be a unit vector in a
K_{2}
-dimensional vector space,

M_{k_{2}}\rightarrow\hat{e}_{k_{2}}.

(337)

For each
k_{1}=1,\ldots,K_{1}
, we consider the state
\rho_{k_{1}}
to be a vector
w_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{2}}
,

\rho_{k_{1}}\rightarrow w_{k_{1}}\equiv\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{K_{2}}w_{k_{1},k_{2}}\hat{e}_{k_{2}},

(338)

where
w_{k_{1},k_{2}}
is an estimate for
\Tr(M_{k_{2}}\rho_{k_{1}})
, such that

\mathrm{Pr}\left[\,\,|w_{k_{1},k_{2}}-\Tr(M_{k_{2}}\rho_{k_{1}})|>\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\,\,\right]\leq\delta.

(339)

We can obtain an estimate for
w_{k_{1},k_{2}}
with the above guarantee using
\mathcal{O}(\log(1/\delta)/\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}^{2})
experiments. We simply prepare
\rho_{k_{1}}
and measure the POVM associated to
M_{k_{2}}
, then compute the average of the indicator function on whether the POVM element
M_{k_{2}}
is the outcome.
Because the indicator function of an event is a bounded random variable, Hoeffding’s inequality gives us the above rigorous guarantee.

J.3
Learning states

For the initial states
\rho_{x}
with
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we represent
\rho_{x}
as a
K_{1}
-dimensional real vector

\rho_{x}\rightarrow v_{x}\equiv\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}v_{x,k_{1}}\hat{e}_{k_{1}},

(340)

where
v_{x}
is an optimum of the following optimization problem

\mathrm{OPT}(v_{x})=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{1}},\left\lVert\alpha\right\rVert_{1}\leq R_{1},\\
\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\alpha_{k_{1}}=1\end{subarray}}\left\lVert\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\alpha_{k_{1}}w_{k_{1}}-\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{K_{2}}v^{\prime}_{x,k_{2}}\hat{e}_{k_{2}}\right\rVert_{1},

(341)

and
v^{\prime}_{x,k_{2}}
is an estimate for
\Tr(M_{k_{2}}\rho_{x})
by doing additional experiments, such that

\mathrm{Pr}\left[\,\,|v^{\prime}_{x,k_{2}}-\Tr(M_{k_{2}}\rho_{x})|>\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}\,\,\right]\leq\delta.

(342)

Because the above optimization is a convex optimization, one could solve for
\alpha_{x}
efficiently.
The purpose of the optimization problem is to project the vector
\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{K_{2}}v^{\prime}_{x,k_{2}}\hat{e}_{k_{2}}
onto the space spanned by
v_{1},\ldots,v_{K_{1}}
.
For a fixed
\tilde{\epsilon}_{v},\delta>0
, the total number of experiments for learning representations of the states is
\mathcal{O}(K_{2}|\mathcal{X}|\log(1/\delta)/\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}_{v})
.

J.4
Learning CPTP maps

For the CPTP maps
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}
with
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, we represent
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}
as a
K_{1}\times K_{1}
real matrix of the form

{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}\rightarrow A_{y}\equiv\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\sum_{k_{1}^{\prime}=1}^{K_{1}}A_{y,k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}\hat{e}_{k_{1}}\hat{e}_{k_{1}^{\prime}}^{T},

(343)

where for each fixed value of
k_{1}^{\prime}
, the
K_{1}
-dimensional vector
A_{y,(\cdot),k_{1}^{\prime}}
is an optimum of the following convex optimization problem

\mathrm{OPT}(A_{y,(\cdot),k_{1}^{\prime}})=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{1}},\left\lVert\alpha\right\rVert_{1}\leq R_{1},\\
\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\alpha_{k_{1}}=1\end{subarray}}\left\lVert\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\alpha_{k_{1}}w_{k_{1}}-\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{K_{2}}A^{\prime}_{y,k_{2},k_{1}^{\prime}}\hat{e}_{k_{2}}\right\rVert_{1},

(344)

and
A^{\prime}_{y,k_{2},k_{1}^{\prime}}
is an estimate for
\Tr(M_{k_{2}}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}(\rho_{k_{1}^{\prime}}))
by doing additional experiments, such that

\mathrm{Pr}\left[\,\,|A^{\prime}_{y,k_{2},k_{1}^{\prime}}-\Tr(M_{k_{2}}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}(\rho_{k_{1}^{\prime}}))|>\tilde{\epsilon}_{A}\,\,\right]\leq\delta.

(345)

Similar to before, the purpose of the optimization is to project the vector
\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{K_{2}}A^{\prime}_{y,k_{2},k_{1}^{\prime}}\hat{e}_{k_{2}}
to the space formed by
v_{1},\ldots,v_{K_{1}}
.
For a fixed
\tilde{\epsilon}_{A},\delta>0
, the total number of experiments for learning representations of the CPTP maps is
\mathcal{O}(K_{1}K_{2}|\mathcal{Y}|\log(1/\delta)/\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}_{A})
.

J.5
Learning POVMs

For the POVM elements
M_{zb}
with
z\in\mathcal{Z},b\in\mathcal{B}
, we represent
M_{zb}
as a
K_{1}
-dimensional real vector

M_{zb}\rightarrow u_{zb}\equiv\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}u_{z,b,k_{1}}\hat{e}_{k_{1}},

(346)

where
u_{z,b,k_{1}}
is an estimate for
\Tr(M_{zb}\rho_{k_{1}})
by doing additional experiments, such that

\mathrm{Pr}\left[\,\,|u_{z,b,k_{1}}-\Tr(M_{zb}\rho_{k_{1}})|>\tilde{\epsilon}_{u}\,\,\right]\leq\delta.

(347)

For a fixed
\tilde{\epsilon}_{u},\delta>0
, the total number of experiments for learning representations of the POVMs is
\mathcal{O}(K_{1}|\mathcal{Z}|\log(1/\delta)/\tilde{\epsilon}_{u}^{2})
.
The reason that we don’t need an extra factor of
|{\mathcal{B}}|
is because when we measure the POVM
\mathcal{M}_{z}
, we can simultaneously estimate
c_{z,b,k_{1}}
for all
b\in\mathcal{B}
.

J.6
Prediction procedure and rigorous guarantee

During the prediction phase, we predict the probability for obtaining an outcome
b\in\mathcal{B}
after running the experiment
E=(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{L},z)
to be

u_{zb}^{T}PA_{y_{L}}P\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x},

(348)

where
P
is the projection to the convex set
\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{1}}|\sum_{k_{1}}\alpha_{k_{1}}=1,\left\lVert\alpha\right\rVert_{1}\leq R_{1}\}
.
We now give a rigorous performance guarantee for this algorithm.

Theorem J.1

(Predicting extrinsic behaviors; Restatement of Theorem
4
)
.

Assume that we have found a complete set of linearly independent states and POVMs.
Using the proposed algorithm, we can predict
\Tr(M_{zb}({{\mathcal{E}}}_{y_{L}}\circ\ldots\circ{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y_{1}})(\rho_{x}))
to
\epsilon
error for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}},b\in{\mathcal{B}}
using a total of

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{|{\mathcal{X}}|+L^{2}|{\mathcal{Y}}|+|{\mathcal{Z}}|}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)

(349)

experiments, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
neglects logarithmic factors and considers
K_{1},K_{2},R_{1},R_{2}
to be constant.

J.7
Proof of Theorem
J.1
— Step A. Representations of target outputs

From Eq. (
333
) and (
334
),
\forall x\in\mathcal{X}
, we can write
\rho_{x}
as

\rho_{x}=\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}v^{*}_{x,k_{1}}\rho_{k_{1}},

(350)

for some
K_{1}
-dimensional vector
v^{*}_{x}
with
\left\lVert v^{*}_{x}\right\rVert_{1}\leq R_{1}
.
Similarly, Eq. (
333
) and (
334
) shows that for any
k_{1}^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,K_{1}\}
, we can write
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}(\rho_{k_{1}^{\prime}})
as

{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}(\rho_{k_{1}^{\prime}})=\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}A^{*}_{y,k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}\rho_{k_{1}},

(351)

for some
K_{1}
-dimensional vector
A^{*}_{y,(\cdot),k_{1}^{\prime}}
with
\left\lVert A^{*}_{y,(\cdot),k_{1}^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{1}\leq R_{1}
.
Using this representation, for an experiment specified by
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}}
, the probability to obtain the measurement outcome
b\in{\mathcal{B}}
can be written as

\Tr(M_{zb}({{\mathcal{E}}}_{y_{L}}\circ\ldots\circ{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y_{1}})(\rho_{x}))=\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}(A^{*}_{y_{L}}\ldots A^{*}_{y_{1}}v^{*}_{x})_{k_{1}}\Tr(M_{zb}\rho_{k_{1}})=(u^{*}_{zb})^{T}A^{*}_{y_{L}}\ldots A^{*}_{y_{1}}v^{*}_{x},

(352)

where we let
u^{*}_{z,b,k_{1}}\equiv\Tr(M_{zb}\rho_{k_{1}})
and
u^{*}_{zb}=\sum_{k_{1}}u^{*}_{z,b,k_{1}}\hat{e}_{k_{1}}
.

J.8
Proof of Theorem
J.1
— Step B. Error analysis for the learned representations

We begin by comparing the two vectors
v_{x}^{*}
and
v_{x}
, where
v_{x}
is the representation learned from experiments; see Eq. (
340
). We can bound the difference as follows,

\displaystyle\left\lVert v_{x}^{*}-v_{x}\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq R_{2}\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{K_{2}}\left|\Tr(M_{k_{2}}\rho_{x})-\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}v_{x,k_{1}}\Tr(M_{k_{2}}\rho_{k_{1}})\right|

(353)

\displaystyle\leq R_{2}\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{K_{2}}\left(\left|v^{\prime}_{x,k_{2}}-\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}v_{x,k_{1}}w_{k1,k2}\right|+\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}+\left\lVert v_{x}\right\rVert_{1}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right)

(354)

\displaystyle=R_{2}\mathrm{OPT}(v_{x})+R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}+\left\lVert v_{x}\right\rVert_{1}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right),

(355)

\displaystyle\leq R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}+\left\lVert v^{*}_{x}\right\rVert_{1}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right)+R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}+\left\lVert v_{x}\right\rVert_{1}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right),

(356)

\displaystyle\leq 2R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}+R_{2}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right),

(357)

with probability at least
1-(K_{2}+1)\delta
.
The first line follows from Eq. (
336
) and (
350
).
The second line follows from Eq. (
339
), (
342
), and the union bound.
The third line follows from Eq. (
341
).
The fourth line follows from considering
\alpha=v^{*}_{x}
in the optimization problem given at Eq. (
341
), and utilizing the following bounds,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}v^{*}_{x,k_{1}}w_{k_{1}}-\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{K_{2}}v^{\prime}_{x,k_{2}}\hat{e}_{k_{2}}\right\rVert_{1}

(358)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{K_{2}}\left|\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}v^{*}_{x,k_{1}}\Tr(M_{k_{2}}\rho_{k_{1}})-\Tr(M_{k_{2}}\rho_{x})\right|+K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}+\left\lVert v^{*}_{x}\right\rVert_{1}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right)

(359)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{K_{2}}\left|\Tr(M_{k_{2}}\rho_{x})-\Tr(M_{k_{2}}\rho_{x})\right|+K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}+\left\lVert v^{*}_{x}\right\rVert_{1}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right)=K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}+\left\lVert v^{*}_{x}\right\rVert_{1}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right).

(360)

The last line follows from
\left\lVert v_{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{1},\left\lVert v_{x}\right\rVert_{1}\leq R_{1}
.

We can compare
A^{*}_{y,k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}
and
A_{y,k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}
by employing the same analysis and replacing Eq. (
350
) with (
351
), Eq. (
340
) with (
343
), Eq. (
342
) with (
345
), and Eq. (
341
) with (
344
).
For all
y\in\mathcal{Y},k_{1}^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,K_{1}\}
, the analysis shows that the following,

\left\lVert A^{*}_{y,(\cdot),k_{1}^{\prime}}-A_{y,(\cdot),k_{1}^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{1}\leq 2R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{A}+R_{2}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right),

(361)

happens with probability at least
1-(K_{2}+1)\delta
.
And recall from Eq. (
347
), we have
|u_{z,b,k_{1}}-u^{*}_{z,b,k_{1}}|\leq\tilde{\epsilon}_{u}
with probability at least
1-\delta
.
Together, with probability at least
1-|{\mathcal{X}}|(K_{2}+1)\delta-|{\mathcal{Y}}|K_{1}(K_{2}+1)\delta-|{\mathcal{Z}}||{\mathcal{B}}|\delta=1-((|{\mathcal{X}}|+K_{1}|{\mathcal{Y}}|)(K_{2}+1)+|{\mathcal{Z}}||{\mathcal{B}}|)\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\left|v^{*}_{x,k_{1}}-v_{x,k_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}+R_{2}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right),

\displaystyle\forall x\in{\mathcal{X}},

(362)

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\left|A^{*}_{y,k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}-A_{y,k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{A}+R_{2}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right),

\displaystyle\forall y\in{\mathcal{Y}},\,\forall k_{1}^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,K_{1}\},

(363)

\displaystyle\left|u^{*}_{z,b,k_{1}}-u_{z,b,k_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\epsilon}_{u},

\displaystyle\forall z\in{\mathcal{Z}},b\in{\mathcal{B}},\,\forall k_{1}\in\{1,\ldots,K_{1}\}.

(364)

This provides a set of error bounds for the learned representations.

J.9
Proof of Theorem
J.1
— Step C. Error analysis for the prediction

We will now analyze the difference between the prediction and the true answer.
We first define a linear function mapping a
K_{1}
-dimensional vector
\alpha
to a matrix

\rho(\alpha)=\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\alpha_{k_{1}}\rho_{k_{1}}.

(365)

Because
\rho_{k_{1}}
is a quantum state, we have

\left\lVert\rho(\alpha)\right\rVert_{1}\leq\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\left|\alpha_{k_{1}}\right|\left\lVert\rho_{k_{1}}\right\rVert_{1}=\left\lVert\alpha\right\rVert_{1}.

(366)

Note that
\left\lVert\rho(\alpha)\right\rVert_{1}
is the trace norm for a matrix, while
\left\lVert\alpha\right\rVert_{1}
is a vector one-norm.
For an experiment specified by
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}}
, the difference between the probability for obtaining
b\in{\mathcal{B}}
and the predicted probability is

\displaystyle\left|u_{zb}^{T}PA_{y_{L}}P\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x}-(u^{*}_{zb})^{T}A^{*}_{y_{L}}\ldots A^{*}_{y_{1}}v^{*}_{x}\right|

(367)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k_{1}}\left|u_{z,b,k_{1}}-u^{*}_{z,b,k_{1}}\right|\left\lVert PA_{y_{L}}P\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x}\right\rVert_{1}

(368)

\displaystyle+|\Tr\left(M_{zb}\rho(PA_{y_{L}}P\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x})\right)-\Tr\left(M_{zb}\rho(A^{*}_{y_{L}}\ldots A^{*}_{y_{1}}v^{*}_{x})\right)|

(369)

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\epsilon}_{u}R_{1}+\left\lVert\rho(PA_{y_{L}}P\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x})-\rho(A^{*}_{y_{L}}\ldots A^{*}_{y_{1}}v^{*}_{x})\right\rVert_{1}.

(370)

The first inequality is a telescoping sum with a triangle inequality.
The second inequality follows from Eq. (
364
) and the fact that
\left\lVert M_{zb}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
.

We will now analyze the second term in the above equation. We will prove that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho(PA_{y_{L}}P\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x})-\rho(A^{*}_{y_{L}}\ldots A^{*}_{y_{1}}v^{*}_{x})\right\rVert_{1}

(371)

\displaystyle\leq 2R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}+R_{2}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right)+2LR_{1}R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{A}+R_{2}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right)

(372)

by induction on
L
.
For the base case
L=0,
from Eq. (
362
) and Eq. (
366
), we see that

\left\lVert\rho(v_{x})-\rho(v^{*}_{x})\right\rVert_{1}\leq\left\lVert v_{x}-v^{*}_{x}\right\rVert_{1}\leq 2R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}+R_{2}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right).

(373)

Suppose that the claim holds for
L-1
. Then

\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho(PA_{y_{L}}P\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x})-\rho(A^{*}_{y_{L}}\ldots A^{*}_{y_{1}}v^{*}_{x})\right\rVert_{1}

(374)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\rho(PA_{y_{L}}(PA_{y_{L-1}}\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x}))-\rho(A^{*}_{y_{L}}(PA_{y_{L-1}}\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x}))\right\rVert_{1}

(375)

\displaystyle+\left\lVert\rho(A^{*}_{y_{L}}(PA_{y_{L-1}}\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x}))-\rho(A^{*}_{y_{L}}(A^{*}_{y_{L-1}}\ldots A^{*}_{y_{1}}v^{*}_{x}))\right\rVert_{1}

(376)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert PA_{y_{L}}(PA_{y_{L-1}}\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x})-A^{*}_{y_{L}}(PA_{y_{L-1}}\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x})\right\rVert_{1}

(377)

\displaystyle+\left\lVert\mathcal{E}_{y_{L}}\big(\rho(PA_{y_{L-1}}\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x}-A^{*}_{y_{L-1}}\ldots A^{*}_{y_{1}}v^{*}_{x})\big)\right\rVert_{1}

(378)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\left(\max_{k_{1}^{\prime}}\left|PA_{y_{L},k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}-A^{*}_{y_{L},k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}\right|\sum_{k_{1}^{\prime}=1}^{K_{1}}\left|(PA_{y_{L-1}}\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x})_{k_{1}^{\prime}}\right|\right)

(379)

\displaystyle+\left\lVert\rho(PA_{y_{L-1}}\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x}-A^{*}_{y_{L-1}}\ldots A^{*}_{y_{1}}v^{*}_{x})\right\rVert_{1}

(380)

\displaystyle\leq 2R_{1}R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{A}+R_{2}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right)

(381)

\displaystyle+2R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}+R_{2}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right)+2(L-1)R_{1}R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{A}+R_{2}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right)

(382)

\displaystyle=2R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}+R_{2}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right)+2LR_{1}R_{2}K_{2}\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{A}+R_{2}\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}\right).

(383)

The first inequality follows from the triangle inequality and the linearity of
\rho(\alpha)
.
The second inequality follows from the action of
\mathcal{E}_{y}
given in Eq. (
351
).
The third inequality uses two basic inequalities:
\left\lVert(A-A^{\prime})x\right\rVert\leq\sum_{i}(\max_{j}|(A-A^{\prime})_{i}j|\sum_{j}x_{j}),\forall A,A^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k},x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\left\lVert\mathcal{E}(X)\right\rVert_{1}\leq\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{1}
for CPTP map
\mathcal{E}
and Hermitian matrix
X
.
The fourth inequality uses the induction hypothesis, Eq. (
363
), and the fact that
\sum_{k}|(Px)_{k}|\leq R_{1}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{1}}
.

J.10
Proof of Theorem
J.1
— Step D. Putting everything together

Together, we consider the following parameter choices,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}

\displaystyle=100((|{\mathcal{X}}|+K_{1}|{\mathcal{Y}}|)(K_{2}+1)+|{\mathcal{Z}}||{\mathcal{B}}|),

(384)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tilde{\epsilon}_{w}}

\displaystyle=\frac{16LR_{1}R_{2}^{2}K_{2}}{\epsilon},

(385)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tilde{\epsilon}_{v}}

\displaystyle=\frac{8R_{2}K_{2}}{\epsilon},

(386)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tilde{\epsilon}_{A}}

\displaystyle=\frac{8LR_{1}R_{2}K_{2}}{\epsilon},

(387)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tilde{\epsilon}_{u}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4R_{1}}{\epsilon},

(388)

to ensure that with probability at least
0.99
, for any experiment specified by
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{1},\ldots,y_{L}\in{\mathcal{Y}},z\in{\mathcal{Z}}
, the difference between the actual probability for obtaining the measurement outcome
b\in{\mathcal{B}}
and the predicted probability is bounded above by
\epsilon
,

\left|u_{zb}^{T}PA_{y_{L}}P\ldots PA_{y_{1}}v_{x}-\Tr(M_{zb}({{\mathcal{E}}}_{y_{L}}\circ\ldots\circ{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y_{1}})(\rho_{x}))\right|\leq\epsilon.

(389)

By aggregating the number of experiments for learning a frame, states, maps, and POVMs,
the total number of experiments is of order

\displaystyle\frac{L^{2}R^{2}_{1}R_{2}^{4}K^{2}_{2}+|{\mathcal{X}}|R_{2}^{2}K_{2}^{3}+L^{2}|{\mathcal{Y}}|K_{1}K^{3}_{2}R^{2}_{1}R^{2}_{2}+|{\mathcal{Z}}|R_{1}^{2}K_{1}}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\left(K_{2}|{\mathcal{X}}|+K_{1}K_{2}|{\mathcal{Y}}|+|{\mathcal{Z}}||{\mathcal{B}}|\right)

(390)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{|{\mathcal{X}}|+L^{2}|{\mathcal{Y}}|+|{\mathcal{Z}}|}{\epsilon^{2}}\right),

(391)

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
neglects logarithmic contributions and considers
K_{1},K_{2},R_{1},R_{2}
to be constant.

APPENDIX K
Related work

In this appendix, we present the connection of the theory developed in this work and existing works. We will refer to basic concepts developed in some of the previous appendices. In particular, Appendix
A
on the definition of world models, Appendix
C
on the definition of model classes and learning intrinsic descriptions, and Appendix
I
on the definition of predicting extrinsic behaviors.

K.1
Gate set tomography

The most relevant literature to the theory of world models developed in this work is gate set tomography.
Here we provide a brief review on gate set tomography.
We refer the readers to two comprehensive reviews on gate set tomography
greenbaum2015introduction
;
nielsen2020gate
. Ref.
greenbaum2015introduction
gives the basic concepts and
nielsen2020gate
provides both the fundamental ideas and recent progress on gate set tomography. An experimental demonstration of gate set tomography is given in
blume2017demonstration
. An open-sourced software for gate set tomography has been developed in
erik_2021_5546759
.

The main goal of gate set tomography (GST) is to characterize how quantum processes and logical gates affect the qubits in the device.
This is closely related to the task of quantum process tomography (QPT).
However, in quantum process tomography, one assumes perfect state preparations and perfect POVM measurements.
The key differences between GST and QPT are: (1) the lack of assumption on perfect states and measurements; (2) the need to benchmark multiple quantum processes (gates) at once.
Because GST does not assume the ability to prepare perfect states and measurements, existing protocols learn the relation between different gates instead of the intrinsic physical description of each gate. This problem is referred to as gauge freedom in GST.

When we vectorize state and POVM element as
\mathrm{vec}(\rho_{x})
and
\mathrm{vec}(M_{zb})
and write the CPTP maps
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}
as a matrix
A_{y}
, then we have

\Tr(M_{zb}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{y}(\rho_{x}))=\mathrm{vec}(M_{zb})^{T}A_{y}\mathrm{vec}(\rho_{x})=(M^{-1}\mathrm{vec}(M_{zb}))^{T}(MA_{y}M^{-1})(M\mathrm{vec}(\rho_{x})).

(392)

The following transformation is known as a gauge transformation

\displaystyle\mathrm{vec}(\rho_{x})

\displaystyle\rightarrow M\mathrm{vec}(\rho_{x}),

(393)

\displaystyle A_{y}

\displaystyle\rightarrow MA_{y}M^{-1},

(394)

\displaystyle\mathrm{vec}(M_{zb})

\displaystyle\rightarrow M^{-1}\mathrm{vec}(M_{zb}),

(395)

which is parameterized by an invertible matrix
M
.
Most existing GST protocols are designed to learn the vectorization and matricization up the the gauge freedom, specified by the matrix
M
.
Due to the gauge
M
, it has been difficult to provide a fully rigorous statistical analysis of GST. The key problem is that the gauge freedom makes it challenging to define errors in GST.
And we are not aware of a rigorous proof showing the required number of experiments to perform GST up to certain error.

We begin with a summary of the difference between the theory on learning world models developed in this work and the existing literature on gate set tomography
nielsen2020gate
.
According to the review
nielsen2020gate
, GST is tomography of a novel entity, which is not the individual description of each gate, but some form of relations between the gates.
Based on our theory, we can formalize this novel entity as a non-physical model capable of predicting the extrinsic behaviors of the quantum device under the following assumption: one can (efficiently) find a complete basis of states and POVMs by composing existing states, gates, and measurements.
Without this assumption, Theorem
3
shows that learning the extrinsic behavior can be extremely inefficient. And under this assumption, Theorem
4
provides a rigorous algorithm, that shares many common aspects with GST, for predicting extrinsic behaviors that improves significantly upon the worst case complexity given in Theorem
3
.

In contrast, learning intrinsic physical descriptions of the operations in the device is significantly more challenging than performing GST. In certain scenarios, it is impossible to learn the intrinsic descriptions to arbitrary accuracy as shown in Theorem
6
.
However, we show that it is possible in many natural scenarios.
We can learn the intrinsic description up to any accuracy when the world model has an unknown pure state and a universal set of unknown gates (Theorem
2
).
Hence, being “informationally complete” for GST is easier than being “informationally complete” for learning intrinsic descriptions.

We now present several questions that illustrate the difference between the theory on learning world models developed in this work and gate set tomography.

Is equivalence up to a gauge transformation in GST the same as weak indistinguishability?

No. When two world models are related by the gauge transformation defined in GST, it is true that the two world models are weakly indistinguishable, i.e., no experiments can distinguish the two world models.
However, there are pairs of weakly indistinguishable world models that are not related by a gauge transformation.
One such example has been presented in the main text. Consider the following two distinct physical realities in a single-qubit system,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{A}_{\mathrm{HT}}:\quad\rho^{A}_{0}

\displaystyle=I/2,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}_{H}(\rho)

\displaystyle=H\rho H^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}_{T}(\rho)

\displaystyle=T\rho T^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{A}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert\},

(396)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{B}_{\mathrm{HT}}:\quad\rho^{B}_{0}

\displaystyle=I/2,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{B}_{H}(\rho)

\displaystyle=I/2,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{B}_{T}(\rho)

\displaystyle=I/2,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{B}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert\},

(397)

with actions
\mathcal{X}=\{0\},\mathcal{Y}=\{H,T\},\mathcal{Z}=\{0\}
.
Because
\mathcal{E}^{A}_{H}\neq\mathcal{E}^{A}_{T}
but
\mathcal{E}^{B}_{H}=\mathcal{E}^{B}_{T}
, it is impossible that the two world models are related by a gauge transformation.
However, as we have discussed in the main text, the two world models cannot be distinguished by any experiment, hence they are weakly indistinguishable.
Together, we see that gauge equivalence as defined in GST implies weak indistinguishability, but two devices that are weakly indistinguishable need not be gauge equivalent in GST.
This means the set of relations defined by gauge equivalence in GST is a subset of the relations defined by weakly indistinguishable world models.

Can we always learn a world model up to gauge transformation?

No. While GST removes the assumption on perfect state preparations and measurements, GST still require assumptions to work. It is not true that we can always learn a world model up to a gauge transformation using existing GST protocols. This is already evident in the above example. The two world models
\mathcal{W}^{A}_{\mathrm{HT}},\mathcal{W}^{B}_{\mathrm{HT}}
are not related by a gauge transformation, but they are weakly indistinguishable.
Hence by Proposition
E.2
, we cannot learn to distinguish the two world models from experiments.

Is equivalence up to a gauge transformation in GST the same as strong indistinguishability (i.e. equivalence in our framework)?

No. As Theorem
A.8
has shown, equivalent world models are related by unitary or anti-unitary transformation. But two world models related by the gauge transformation defined in GST may not be related by unitary or anti-unitary transformation.
For example, consider two
d
-dimensional world models over the action spaces
\mathcal{X}=\{\sigma\}_{\sigma:\mathrm{state}},\mathcal{Y}=\{U\}_{U\in\mathrm{SU}(d)},\mathcal{Z}=\{0\}
and outcome space
{\mathcal{B}}=\{1,\ldots,d\}
:

\mathcal{W}^{A}=\left(\{\rho^{A}_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}},\{\mathcal{E}^{A}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{Y}},\{\mathcal{M}^{A}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\right)
, where

\displaystyle\rho^{A}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=(1-\epsilon)\sigma+\epsilon\frac{I}{d},

\displaystyle\forall\sigma:\mathrm{state},

(398)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}_{U}(\rho)

\displaystyle=U\rho U^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\forall U\in\mathrm{SU}(d),

(399)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{A}_{0}

\displaystyle=\left\{\lvert b\rangle\!\langle b\rvert\right\}_{b=1,\ldots,d},

(400)

and
\mathcal{W}^{B}=\left(\{\rho^{B}_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}},\{\mathcal{E}^{B}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{Y}},\{\mathcal{M}^{B}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\right)
, where

\displaystyle\rho^{B}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\sigma,

\displaystyle\forall\sigma:\mathrm{state},

(401)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{B}_{U}(\rho)

\displaystyle=U\rho U^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\forall U\in\mathrm{SU}(d),

(402)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{B}_{0}

\displaystyle=\left\{(1-\epsilon)\lvert b\rangle\!\langle b\rvert+\epsilon\frac{I}{d}\right\}_{b=1,\ldots,d},

(403)

We can see that
\mathcal{W}^{A}
has depolarized initial states, while
\mathcal{W}^{B}
has depolarized measurements.
The two world models are not related by a unitary or anti-unitary transformation, hence they describe distinct physical realities.
However, it is not hard to show that the two world models are related by a gauge transformation
M
.

Are two gauge-equivalent devices always physically the same?

No. We can have two noisy devices with depolarization noise happening in the states or in the measurements; see the above example of
\mathcal{W}^{A}
and
\mathcal{W}^{B}
. The two devices are gauge equivalent. But these two noise processes are not physically the same.
And hence the two devices are not physically the same.
The inability to distinguish the two world models
\mathcal{W}^{A}
and
\mathcal{W}^{B}
is due to the lack of sufficiently informative actions.
This is similar to the case when one performs quantum state tomography with computational basis measurements.
A coherent
\lvert+\rangle\!\langle+\rvert
state is indistinguishable from a maximally mixed state
I/2
under computational basis measurements. However, the two states are not physically the same.
The indistinguishability arises not from being physically the same, but from the lack of sufficiently useful actions.

How does gauge equivalence defined in GST relate to our theory of world models?

Suppose that we have a
d
-dimensional world model
\mathcal{W}
, and assume we can identify a set of (composed) states and a set of (composed) POVM elements, such that both sets span the entire
d
-dimensional quantum state space.
Then every world model
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
that is weakly indistinguishable from
\mathcal{W}
is related by a gauge transformation.
Hence, under this assumption, we can equate weak indistinguishability in our framework and gauge equivalence in GST.
The assumption is critical and has been explicitly or implicitly assumed in existing GST protocols
nielsen2020gate
.
A similar assumption is made in Theorem
4
(restated in Theorem
J.1
) to establish the existence of an efficient algorithm for learning extrinsic behaviors.

K.2
Quantum state/process/measurement tomography

The settings studied in quantum state, process, and measurement tomography are special cases of our theory.
In quantum state tomography, we are learning a world model such that the CPTP maps and POVM measurements are perfect.
Quantum state tomography focuses on learning an unknown quantum state
\rho
to high accuracy, usually in trace norm or fidelity — see e.g.
hradil1997quantum
;
gross2010quantum
;
blume2010optimal
;
banaszek2013focus
and references therein.
Recently, there have also been works investigating how learning theory can be used to provide new insights into quantum state tomography
aaronson2018online
;
quek2021private
;
buadescu2021improved
;
huang2020predicting
.
In quantum measurement tomography
d2003quantum
, we are learning a world model such that the state preparations and the the CPTP maps are perfect.
The goal of quantum measurement tomography is to learn the descriptions of the POVMs.
Finally, in quantum process tomography
mohseni2008quantum
, we are learning a world model such that the state preparations and the POVM measurements are perfect.
The purpose of quantum process tomography is to learn the full description of some unknown quantum processes.
A subset of quantum processes that have been actively studied recently are the Pauli channels
harper2020efficient
;
flammia2020efficient
;
flammia2021pauli
, which are often considered to be a good description of the noise in quantum computers.

APPENDIX L
Quantum advantage with noisy devices that cannot be fully learned

L.1
Setting

We are given an
(2n)
-qubit device with unknown operations.

L.1.a
State preparation and measurements

We can prepare an unknown product state given by

\rho_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\rho_{2n}.

(404)

We can perform an unknown product measurement given by a POVM with
2^{2n}
outcomes,

\left\{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2n}M^{(i)}_{B_{i}}\right\}_{B\in\{0,1\}^{2n}},

(405)

where
M^{(i)}_{B_{i}},\forall i=1,\ldots,2n
are
2\times 2
positive-semidefinite matrices.

L.1.b
Operations

In the device, we can apply layers of non-overlapping single- and two-qubit gates.
We consider the
2n
qubits to be represented by an
n\times 2
grid.
The grid coordinate of the
i
-th qubit (
i
from
1
to
n
) is
(\lceil i/2\rceil,i\,\,\mathrm{mod}\,\,2)
.
Single-qubit gates can be applied at every grid point.
But two-qubit gates can only be applied at edges on the
n\times 2
grid.
Each single-qubit gate is an unknown CPTP map on the corresponding single qubit.
And each two-qubit gate is an unknown CPTP map on the corresponding two qubits.
The unknown CPTP map implemented by each gate may depend on the presence or absence of all the other gates applied at the same layer.

Finally, we assume that we can load some unknown
n
-qubit quantum state
\rho
on the
n
qubits in the left hand side of the
n\times 2
grid.
Suppose
\sigma
is the quantum state on the
2n
qubits.
After loading
\rho
, the state on the
2n
qubits becomes
\rho\otimes\Tr_{\mathrm{left\,\,side}}(\sigma)
.

L.2
Algorithm for partially learning the device

In this section, we will partially learn some of the operations in the unknown quantum device. We will obtain some descriptions that are useful for using the quantum device to achieve advantage in performing entangled data analysis.
The descriptions do not fully characterize the device.
But the lack of full characterization enables us to work under a more general setting.

L.2.a
Experiments and loss functions: Single-qubit

We perform an optimization to find nine single-qubit gates for every qubit, denoted as
g_{i,k}
for all
i=1,\ldots,2n,k=0,\ldots,8
, by minimizing a loss function.
In particular, after choosing
g_{i,k}
, we conduct the following experiments.

We consider the same single-qubit gates on the left hand side and the right hand side of the
n\times 2
qubit grid.
For each of
k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}\in\{0,\ldots,5\},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}\in\{6,7,8\}
, we prepare the unknown product state
\rho_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\rho_{2n}
, apply a layer of single-qubit gates, followed by another layer of single-qubit gates, then measure using the unknown product measurement.
In the first layer of the single-qubit gates, we apply
g_{i,k_{1}}
for qubit
i
on the left and apply
g_{i,k_{1}^{\prime}}
for qubit
i
on the right.
In the second layer of the single-qubit gates, we apply
g_{i,k_{2}}
for qubit
i
on the left and apply
g_{i,k_{2}^{\prime}}
for qubit
i
on the right.
For each
k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}
and each qubit
i
, we estimate the probability for obtaining the outcome
b\in\{0,1\}
, denoted as
\hat{p}_{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},i,b}
.
From a total of
\mathcal{O}(\log(n/\delta)/\eta_{0}^{2})
such experiments, the estimate
\hat{p}_{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},i,b}
is equal to the true probability up to
\eta_{0}
error for all
k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},i,b
with a probability at least
1-\delta
.
We now define a loss function based on the estimated values,

\eta_{1}=\max_{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},i,b}\left|\hat{p}_{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},i,b}-f(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},i,b)\right|.

(406)

The function
f(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},i,b)
is defined as follows.
For qubit
i
on the left, we have

f(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},i,b)=\begin{cases}1/2&\lfloor k_{1}/2\rfloor\neq(k_{2}-6)\\
1&\lfloor k_{1}/2\rfloor=(k_{2}-6),\,\,k_{1}\equiv b\,(\mathrm{mod}2)\\
0&\lfloor k_{1}/2\rfloor=(k_{2}-6),\,\,k_{1}\not\equiv b\,(\mathrm{mod}2)\\
\end{cases}

(407)

For qubit
i
on the right, we have

f(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},i,b)=\begin{cases}1/2&\lfloor k_{1}^{\prime}/2\rfloor\neq(k_{2}^{\prime}-6)\\
1&\lfloor k_{1}^{\prime}/2\rfloor=(k_{2}^{\prime}-6),\,\,k_{1}^{\prime}\equiv b\,(\mathrm{mod}2)\\
0&\lfloor k_{1}^{\prime}/2\rfloor=(k_{2}^{\prime}-6),\,\,k_{1}^{\prime}\not\equiv b\,(\mathrm{mod}2)\\
\end{cases}

(408)

We optimize over the selection of gates such that the loss function
\eta_{1}
is as small as possible.

L.2.b
Rigorous guarantee: Single-qubit

Given an estimation error
\eta_{0}
and the loss function
\eta_{1}
, we can approximately learn the following states and measurements.
We combine the first layer of single-qubit gates determined by
k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}
and the unknown product state to create a new set of product states, denoted as

\rho^{(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime})}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\rho^{(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime})}_{2n},

(409)

for all
k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}\in\{0,1,2,3,4,5\}
.
We also combine the last layer of single-qubit gates determined by
k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}
and the unknown product measurement to create a new set of product measurements,

\left\{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2n}M^{(i,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime})}_{B_{i}}\right\}_{B\in\{0,1\}^{2n}},

(410)

for all
k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}\in\{6,7,8\}
.
We have the following characterization.

Lemma L.1

(Single-qubit stabilizer states)
.

For any
i=1,\ldots,2n
, there exists a unitary or anti-unitary transformation
U_{i}
on qubit
i
, such that the following holds for any
k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},b
.
For qubit
i
on the left, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho_{i}^{(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime})}-U_{i}\sigma_{k_{1}}U_{i}^{-1}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\mathcal{O}(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}),

(411)

\displaystyle\left\lVert M^{(i,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime})}_{b}-U_{i}\sigma_{2(k_{2}-6)+b}U_{i}^{-1}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\mathcal{O}(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}).

(412)

For qubit
i
on the right, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho_{i}^{(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime})}-U_{i}\sigma_{k_{1}^{\prime}}U_{i}^{-1}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\mathcal{O}(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}),

(413)

\displaystyle\left\lVert M^{(i,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime})}_{b}-U_{i}\sigma_{2(k_{2}^{\prime}-6)+b}U_{i}^{-1}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\mathcal{O}(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}),

(414)

where the pure states
\sigma_{x}
for
x=0,\ldots,5
are given by

\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,\,\,\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert,\,\,\lvert+\rangle\!\langle+\rvert,\,\,\lvert-\rangle\!\langle-\rvert,\,\,\lvert y+\rangle\!\langle y+\rvert,\,\,\lvert y-\rangle\!\langle y-\rvert.

(415)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we consider qubit
i
on the left hand side of the
n\times 2
grid. The proof is the same for qubits on the right side except that we need to swap
k_{1}\leftrightarrow k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}\leftrightarrow k_{2}^{\prime}
.
From the estimate
\hat{p}_{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},i,b}
and the definition of the loss function
\eta_{1}
,

\left|\Tr\left(M^{(i,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime})}_{b}\rho_{i}^{(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime})}\right)-f(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},i,b)\right|\leq\eta_{0}+\eta_{1},

(416)

for all
k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},b
. The above defines an approximate geometry that can be used to infer the underlying operations. This technique is used in Appendix
D.5
to learn general
d
-dimensional systems.

For a fixed choice of
k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}
, we have six matrices for
\rho_{i}^{(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime})}
and six matrices for
M^{(i,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime})}_{b}
corresponding to different
k_{1},k_{2}
and
b
.
Now, given
q=0,1,2
.
We denote the two matrices
\rho_{i}^{(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime})}
associated to
k_{1}=2q+0,2q+1
as
\rho_{0},\rho_{1}
, denote the two matrices
M^{(i,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime})}_{b}
associated to
k_{2}=q
and
b=0,1
as
M_{0},M_{1}
, and define
\eta=\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}
.
We have the following inequalities from Eq. (
416
),

\Tr(M_{0}\rho_{0})\geq 1-\eta,\,\,\,\Tr(M_{0}\rho_{1})\leq\eta,\,\,\,\Tr(M_{1}\rho_{0})\leq\eta,\,\,\,\Tr(M_{1}\rho_{1})\geq 1-\eta.

(417)

Using the fact that
M_{0},M_{1},\rho_{0},\rho_{1}
are positive-semidefinite,
M_{0}+M_{1}=I,
and
\Tr(\rho_{0})=\Tr(\rho_{1})=1
, there exists two orthogonal pure states
\lvert\psi_{0}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{0}\rvert,\lvert\psi_{1}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{1}\rvert
and a constant
C>0
, such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert M_{0}-\lvert\psi_{0}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{0}\rvert\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C\eta,

\displaystyle\,\,\left\lVert M_{1}-\lvert\psi_{1}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{1}\rvert\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C\eta,

(418)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho_{0}-\lvert\psi_{0}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{0}\rvert\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C\eta,

\displaystyle\,\,\left\lVert\rho_{1}-\lvert\psi_{1}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{1}\rvert\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C\eta.

(419)

Hence, we know that the six matrices for
\rho_{i}^{(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime})}
are approximately pure states.
Then we can use the approximate geometry given in Eq. (
416
) over pairs of distinct
q
to show that there exists a constant
C^{\prime}>0
and a unitary or anti-unitary transformation
U_{i,k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}}
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho_{i}^{(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime})}-U_{i,k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}}\sigma_{k_{1}}U_{i,k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}}^{-1}\right\rVert_{1}\leq C^{\prime}\eta,

(420)

\displaystyle\left\lVert M^{(i,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime})}_{b}-U_{i,k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}}\sigma_{2(k_{2}-6)+b}U_{i,k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}}^{-1}\right\rVert_{1}\leq C^{\prime}\eta,

(421)

where the pure states
\sigma_{x}
for
x=0,\ldots,5
are given by

\sigma_{x}=\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert,\lvert+\rangle\!\langle+\rvert,\lvert-\rangle\!\langle-\rvert,\lvert y+\rangle\!\langle y+\rvert,\lvert y-\rangle\!\langle y-\rvert,

(422)

respectively.

We see that
U_{i,k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}}
depends on
k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}
. The last step is to show that we can actually choose a single unitary or anti-unitary transformation
U_{i}
.
Consider
U_{i}=U_{i,k_{1}^{\prime}=0,k_{2}^{\prime}=6}
. For all
x=0,\ldots,5
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert U_{i,k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}}\sigma_{x}U_{i,k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}}^{-1}-U_{i}\sigma_{x}U_{i}^{-1}\right\rVert_{1}

(423)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert U_{i,k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}}\sigma_{x}U_{i,k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}}^{-1}-U_{i,0,k_{2}^{\prime}}\sigma_{x}U_{i,0,k_{2}^{\prime}}^{-1}\right\rVert_{1}+\left\lVert U_{i,0,k_{2}^{\prime}}\sigma_{x}U_{i,0,k_{2}^{\prime}}^{-1}-U_{i,0,6}\sigma_{x}U_{i,0,6}^{-1}\right\rVert_{1}

(424)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert U_{i,k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}}\sigma_{x}U_{i,k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}}^{-1}-\rho_{i}^{(x,k_{1}^{\prime})}\right\rVert_{1}+\left\lVert\rho_{i}^{(x,k_{1}^{\prime})}-U_{i,0,k_{2}^{\prime}}\sigma_{x}U_{i,0,k_{2}^{\prime}}^{-1}\right\rVert_{1}

(425)

\displaystyle+\left\lVert U_{i,0,k_{2}^{\prime}}\sigma_{x}U_{i,0,k_{2}^{\prime}}^{-1}-M^{(i,k_{2},\lfloor x/2\rfloor+6)}_{x\,\mathrm{mod}\,2}\right\rVert_{1}+\left\lVert M^{(i,k_{2},\lfloor x/2\rfloor+6)}_{x\,\mathrm{mod}\,2}-U_{i,0,6}\sigma_{x}U_{i,0,6}^{-1}\right\rVert_{1}

(426)

\displaystyle\leq 2C^{\prime}\eta+2C^{\prime}\eta=4C^{\prime}\eta.

(427)

The second-to-last inequality follows from Eq. (
420
) and (
421
).
Together, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho_{i}^{(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime})}-U_{i}\sigma_{k_{1}}U_{i}^{-1}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\mathcal{O}(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}),

(428)

\displaystyle\left\lVert M^{(i,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime})}_{b}-U_{i}\sigma_{2(k_{2}-6)+b}U_{i}^{-1}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\mathcal{O}(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}),

(429)

This concludes the proof.
∎

L.2.c
Experiments and loss functions: Two-qubit

We also perform another layer of optimization to find two two-qubit gates for every pair of corresponding qubits on the left and right, i.e., qubit
i=2\ell-1,2\ell
, where
\ell=1,\ldots,n
.
We denote the two two-qubit gates as
g^{(2)}_{\ell,s}
and
g^{(2)}_{\ell,e}
for each
\ell=0,\ldots,n
.
Similar to before, after choosing
g^{(2)}_{\ell,s}
and
g^{(2)}_{\ell,e}
, we conduct the following set of experiments.

For each of
k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}\in\{0,\ldots,5\},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}\in\{6,7,8\},x\in\{s,e\}
, we prepare the unknown product state
\rho_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\rho_{2n}
, apply a layer of single-qubit gates, a layer of two-qubit gates, followed by another layer of single-qubit gates, then measure using the unknown product measurement.
In the first layer of the single-qubit gates, we apply
g_{i,k_{1}}
for qubit
i
on the left and apply
g_{i,k_{1}^{\prime}}
for qubit
i
on the right.
In the middle layer of two-qubit gates, we apply
g^{(2)}_{\ell,x}
on qubit
2\ell-1
on the left and qubit
2\ell
on the right.
In the other layer of the single-qubit gates, we apply
g_{i,k_{2}}
for qubit
i
on the left and apply
g_{i,k_{2}^{\prime}}
for qubit
i
on the right.
For each
k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}
and each pair of qubits
2\ell-1,2\ell
, we estimate the probability for obtaining the two bits
b,b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
as the outcome, denoted as
\hat{p}^{(2)}_{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},x,\ell,b,b^{\prime}}
.
From a total of
\mathcal{O}(\log(n/\delta)/\eta_{0}^{2})
, the estimate
\hat{p}^{(2)}_{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},x,\ell,b,b^{\prime}}
is equal to the true probability up to
\eta_{0}
error for all
k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},x,\ell,b,b^{\prime}
with a probability at least
1-\delta
.
We now define another loss function based on the estimated values,

\eta_{2}=\max_{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},x,\ell,b,b^{\prime}}\left|\hat{p}^{(2)}_{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},x,\ell,b,b^{\prime}}-h(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},x,b,b^{\prime})\right|.

(430)

The function
h(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},x,b,b^{\prime})
is defined as follows.

\displaystyle h(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},x,b,b^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Tr\left((\rho^{\mathrm{(out)}}_{k_{2},b}\otimes\rho^{\mathrm{(out)}}_{k_{2}^{\prime},b^{\prime}})U_{x}(\rho^{\mathrm{(in)}}_{k_{1}}\otimes\rho^{\mathrm{(in)}}_{k_{1}^{\prime}})U_{x}^{\dagger}\right),

(431)

\displaystyle\rho^{\mathrm{(in)}}_{k_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k_{1}},

(432)

\displaystyle\rho^{\mathrm{(out)}}_{k_{2},b}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2(k_{2}-6)+b},

(433)

\displaystyle\sigma_{0},\ldots,\sigma_{5}

\displaystyle=\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert,\lvert+\rangle\!\langle+\rvert,\lvert-\rangle\!\langle-\rvert,\lvert y+\rangle\!\langle y+\rvert,\lvert y-\rangle\!\langle y-\rvert,

(434)

\displaystyle U_{s},U_{e}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{SWAP},\mathrm{BELL},

(435)

where
\mathrm{SWAP}
and
\mathrm{BELL}
are two-qubit unitaries,
\mathrm{SWAP}
swaps the left and right qubits,
\mathrm{BELL}=(H\otimes I)\,\,\mathrm{CNOT}
,
\mathrm{CNOT}
is controlled on the same qubit that the Hadamard
H
acts on.
The entangling operation
\mathrm{BELL}
followed by computational basis measurement is one way to perform Bell measurement.

L.2.d
Rigorous guarantee: Two-qubit

Next, using the bound on the estimation error
\eta_{0}
and the loss function
\eta_{2}
, we can approximately learn the entangling operations.
We can write the middle layer of two-qubit gates as

{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell=1,x}\otimes\ldots\otimes{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell=n,x},

(436)

for
x\in\{s,e\}
, where
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,x}
acts on two qubits,
2\ell-1
and
2\ell
.

Lemma L.2

(SWAP and Bell measurement)
.

For each qubit
i=1,\ldots,2n
, consider the unitary or anti-unitary transformation
U_{i}
on qubit
i
given in Lemma
L.1
.

\displaystyle\left\lVert{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,s}(\cdot)-(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})\mathrm{SWAP}(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})^{-1}(\cdot)(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})\mathrm{SWAP}(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})^{-1}\right\rVert_{1\rightarrow 1}

(437)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}),

(438)

\displaystyle\left\lVert{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,e}(\cdot)-(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})\mathrm{BELL}(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})^{-1}(\cdot)(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})\mathrm{BELL}^{\dagger}(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})^{-1}\right\rVert_{1\rightarrow 1}

(439)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}),

(440)

where
\left\lVert{{\mathcal{E}}}\right\rVert_{1\rightarrow 1}=\max_{\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{1}=1}\left\lVert{{\mathcal{E}}}(X)\right\rVert_{1}
.

Proof.

We prove this lemma for
\mathrm{SWAP}
. The proof for
\mathrm{BELL}
is basically the same.
We begin by defining the following notations.

\displaystyle\rho^{(in)}

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{k_{1}}\otimes\sigma_{k_{1}^{\prime})}),

(441)

\displaystyle\rho^{(out)}

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{2(k_{2}-6)+b}\otimes\sigma_{2(k_{2}^{\prime}-6)+b^{\prime}}),

(442)

\displaystyle\rho^{(in,U)}

\displaystyle=(U_{2\ell-1}\sigma_{k_{1}}U_{2\ell-1}^{-1})\otimes(U_{2\ell}\sigma_{k_{1}^{\prime}}U_{2\ell}^{-1}),

(443)

\displaystyle\rho^{(out,U)}

\displaystyle=(U_{2\ell-1}\sigma_{2(k_{2}-6)+b}U_{2\ell-1}^{-1})\otimes(U_{2\ell}\sigma_{2(k_{2}^{\prime}-6)+b^{\prime}}U_{2\ell}^{-1}),

(444)

\displaystyle\sigma_{0},\ldots,\sigma_{5}

\displaystyle=\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert,\lvert+\rangle\!\langle+\rvert,\lvert-\rangle\!\langle-\rvert,\lvert y+\rangle\!\langle y+\rvert,\lvert y-\rangle\!\langle y-\rvert.

(445)

We can use triangle inequality in Eq. (
430
) to show that

\displaystyle\left|\Tr\left(\left(M^{2\ell-1,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}}_{b}\otimes M^{2\ell,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}}_{b^{\prime}}\right){{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,s}\left(\rho^{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}_{2\ell-1}\otimes\rho^{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}_{2\ell}\right)\right)-\Tr\left(\rho^{(out)}\mathrm{SWAP}\left(\rho^{(in)}\right)\mathrm{SWAP}\right)\right|

(446)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\Tr\left(\left(M^{2\ell-1,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}}_{b}\otimes M^{2\ell,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}}_{b^{\prime}}\right){{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,s}\left(\rho^{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}_{2\ell-1}\otimes\rho^{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}_{2\ell}\right)\right)-\hat{p}^{(2)}_{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},x,\ell,b,b^{\prime}}\right|

(447)

\displaystyle+\left|\hat{p}^{(2)}_{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime},x,\ell,b,b^{\prime}}-\Tr\left(\rho^{(out)}\mathrm{SWAP}\left(\rho^{(in)}\right)\mathrm{SWAP}\right)\right|

(448)

\displaystyle\leq\eta_{0}+\eta_{2}.

(449)

Then from triangle inequality and Lemma
L.1
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\Tr\left(\left(M^{2\ell-1,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}}_{b}\otimes M^{2\ell,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}}_{b^{\prime}}\right){{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,s}\left(\rho^{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}_{2\ell-1}\otimes\rho^{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}_{2\ell}\right)\right)-\Tr\left(\rho^{(out,U)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,s}\left(\rho^{(in,U)}\right)\right)\right|

(450)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\Tr\left(\left(M^{2\ell-1,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}}_{b}\otimes M^{2\ell,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}}_{b^{\prime}}\right){{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,s}\left(\rho^{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}_{2\ell-1}\otimes\rho^{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}_{2\ell}\right)\right)-\Tr\left(\rho^{(out,U)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,s}\left(\rho^{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}_{2\ell-1}\otimes\rho^{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}_{2\ell}\right)\right)\right|

(451)

\displaystyle+\left|\Tr\left(\rho^{(out,U)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,s}\left(\rho^{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}_{2\ell-1}\otimes\rho^{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}_{2\ell}\right)\right)-\Tr\left(\rho^{(out,U)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,s}\left(\rho^{(in,U)}\right)\right)\right|

(452)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\left(M^{2\ell-1,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}}_{b}\otimes M^{2\ell,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}}_{b^{\prime}}\right)-\rho^{(out,U)}\right\rVert_{1}+\left\lVert\rho^{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}_{2\ell-1}\otimes\rho^{k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}}_{2\ell}-\rho^{(in,U)}\right\rVert_{1}

(453)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}).

(454)

Hence, by another triangle inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\Tr\left(\rho^{(out,U)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,s}\left(\rho^{(in,U)}\right)\right)-\Tr\left(\rho^{(out)}\mathrm{SWAP}\left(\rho^{(in)}\right)\mathrm{SWAP}\right)\right|\leq\mathcal{O}(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}).

(455)

Because every
(2\times 2)\times(2\times 2)
Hermitian matrix can be written as a linear combination of
\sigma_{i}\otimes\sigma_{j},\forall i,j\in\{0,1,\ldots,5\}
, we have the following bound by triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\left\lVert{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,s}(\cdot)-(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})\mathrm{SWAP}(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})^{-1}(\cdot)(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})\mathrm{SWAP}(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})^{-1}\right\rVert_{1\rightarrow 1}

(456)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}).

(457)

This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 5
.

If
\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}
is smaller than some constant, then
U_{2\ell-1}
and
U_{2\ell}
must be both unitary or both anti-unitary, which can be shown by a proof by contradiction.

L.2.e
Putting everything together

When the quantum device is designed perfectly, the loss functions vanish, i.e.,
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}=0
.
However, when the quantum device is subject to some unknown noise, the loss functions
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
will be small but non-zero.
Here, we show that when we perform sufficiently many experiments to estimate the loss functions and we find the loss functions to be small, then we can guarantee that the underlying physical operations satisfy a certain form.

From now on, we assume that
\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}
are small enough such that there exists
\epsilon>0
and a set of unitary or anti-unitary transformations
U_{i},\forall i=1,\ldots,2n
satisfying the following constraints.
For all
\ell=1,\ldots,n
,
k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}\in\{0,\ldots,5\}
, and
k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}\in\{6,7,8\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho_{2\ell-1}^{(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime})}\otimes\rho_{2\ell}^{(k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime})}-(U_{2\ell-1}\sigma_{k_{1}}U_{2\ell}^{-1})\otimes(U_{2\ell}\sigma_{k_{1}^{\prime}}U_{2\ell}^{-1})\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon,

(458)

\displaystyle\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}}\left\lVert M^{(2\ell-1,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime})}_{b}\otimes M^{(2\ell,k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime})}_{b^{\prime}}-(U_{2\ell-1}\sigma_{2(k_{2}-6)+b}U_{2\ell}^{-1})\otimes(U_{2\ell}\sigma_{2(k_{2}^{\prime}-6)+b^{\prime}}U_{2\ell}^{-1})\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon,

(459)

where the pure states
\sigma_{x}
for
x=0,\ldots,5
are given by

\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,\,\,\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert,\,\,\lvert+\rangle\!\langle+\rvert,\,\,\lvert-\rangle\!\langle-\rvert,\,\,\lvert y+\rangle\!\langle y+\rvert,\,\,\lvert y-\rangle\!\langle y-\rvert.

(460)

And we also have

\displaystyle\left\lVert{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,s}-(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})\mathrm{SWAP}(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})^{-1}(\cdot)(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})\mathrm{SWAP}(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})^{-1}\right\rVert_{1\rightarrow 1}\leq\epsilon,

(461)

\displaystyle\left\lVert{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell,e}-(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})\mathrm{BELL}(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})^{-1}(\cdot)(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})\mathrm{BELL}^{\dagger}(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})^{-1}\right\rVert_{1\rightarrow 1}\leq\epsilon,

(462)

where
\mathrm{SWAP}
and
\mathrm{BELL}
are two-qubit unitaries,
\mathrm{SWAP}
swaps the left and right qubits,
\mathrm{BELL}=(H\otimes I)\,\,\mathrm{CNOT}
, and
\left\lVert{{\mathcal{E}}}\right\rVert_{1\rightarrow 1}=\max_{\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{1}=1}\left\lVert{{\mathcal{E}}}(X)\right\rVert_{1}
.

L.3
Task description and quantum advantage

We focus on the task of predicting many incompatible properties in unknown physical systems studied in
huang2021information
;
chen2021exponential
;
huang2021quantum
.
We consider an unknown physical system described by an
n
-qubit separable state
\rho
. Recall that a separable state is a classical probability mixture over product states.
The goal is to learn to predict properties about
\rho
.

We compare two experimental settings: conventional experiments and quantum-enhanced experiments.
In
conventional experiments
, the physicist could perform any POVM measurement on the unknown state
\rho
to gather classical data. Based on the measurement data, the physicist could adaptively choose the next measurement on
\rho
to obtain more data.
After many rounds of measurements, the physicist combines all measurement outcomes to form a model about the unknown state
\rho
.
In
quantum-enhanced experiments
, the physicist could load multiple copies of the unknown state
\rho
to a quantum computing system. The physicist can then use the quantum computer to perform quantum data analysis to learn a model about the unknown state
\rho
.
After learning a model of
\rho
, we will ask the physicist to predict properties of
\rho
.

We assume that the conventional experiments are perfect. All the POVM measurement could be chosen arbitrarily and are not subject to any noise.
In contrast, we consider quantum-enhanced experiments to base only on the noisy and unknown quantum device that we partially learned in Appendix
L.2
.
Despite the imperfection of quantum-enhanced experiments, we can still demonstrate a large quantum advantage.

Theorem L.3

(Advantage with noisy quantum device; Restatement of Theorem
7
)
.

Given
n>0
. Suppose
\epsilon
is the error on each two-qubit operation in the noisy quantum device. There exists a distribution over unknown
n
-qubit separable states
\rho
and properties, such that quantum-enhanced experiments using the noisy quantum device only require
N_{\mathrm{Q}}=\mathcal{O}((1/(1-4\epsilon))^{2n})
experiments to predict accurately, while noiseless conventional experiments require
N_{\mathrm{C}}=\Omega(2^{n})
experiments. This yields a separation of
N_{\mathrm{C}}=\Omega(N_{\mathrm{Q}}^{a}),
where
a=-\log(2)/(2\log(1-4\epsilon))=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
.

L.4
A class of states and properties

For certain states and properties, conventional experiments can be very powerful.
For example, classical shadow tomography
huang2020predicting
;
huang2022learning
is a class of conventional experiments based on randomized measurements that can be used to make accurate prediction for many properties.
Here, we give an example where there is a large separation between conventional experiments with perfect measurements and noisy quantum-enhanced experiments.
We consider a distribution over the unknown
n
-qubit separable states
\rho
and the properties as follows.

With probability
1/(4^{n}-1)
, we sample a Pauli observable
P\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}\setminus\{I^{\otimes n}\}
.
Then with probability
1/2
, we consider the unknown state
\rho
to be the maximally mixed state
I/2^{n}
.
With probability
1/4
, we consider the unknown state
\rho
to be

\left(\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{n}U_{2\ell-1}\right)\left(\frac{I+0.9P}{2^{n}}\right)\left(\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{n}U_{2\ell-1}^{-1}\right).

(463)

With probability
1/4
, we consider the unknown state
\rho
to be

\left(\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{n}U_{2\ell-1}\right)\left(\frac{I-0.9P}{2^{n}}\right)\left(\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{n}U_{2\ell-1}^{-1}\right).

(464)

The property we would like to predict is the absolute value of the expectation value of

O=\left(\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{n}U_{2\ell-1}\right)P\left(\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{n}U_{2\ell-1}^{-1}\right).

(465)

We tell the learning algorithm both the Pauli observable
P
and the above observable
O
.
When
\rho=I/2^{n}
, the property is equal to zero.
However, when
\rho
is an alternative state that is not
I/2^{n}
, the property is equal to
0.9
.
Hence, making accurate prediction in this task is equivalent to distinguishing whether
\rho
is the maximally mixed state
I/2^{n}
.

L.4.a
Characterization of the probability distribution

Both the states
I/2^{n}
and
(I\pm 0.9P)/2^{n}
are separable states and can be represented as a classical probability distribution over tensor products of the single-qubit stabilizer states

S=\big\{\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,\,\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert,\,\lvert+\rangle\!\langle+\rvert,\,\lvert-\rangle\!\langle-\rvert,\,\lvert y+\rangle\!\langle y+\rvert,\,\lvert y-\rangle\!\langle y-\rvert\big\}.

(466)

The physical system
\rho
is a classical probability mixture over tensor products of
U_{2\ell-1}\sigma_{\ell}U_{2\ell-1}^{-1}
for
\ell=1,\ldots,n
, where
\sigma_{\ell}
is a single-qubit stabilizer state. Hence, we have

\rho=\sum_{\sigma_{\ell}\in S,\forall\ell=1,\ldots,n}p(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})(U_{1}\sigma_{1}U_{1}^{-1})\otimes\ldots\otimes(U_{2n-1}\sigma_{n}U_{2n-1}^{-1}).

(467)

There are multiple distinct probability distributions that lead to the same state
\rho
.
Here, we consider the following classical distribution based on the chosen Pauli observable
P\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}\setminus\{I^{\otimes n}\}
for the ease of analysis.

•

\rho
is the maximally mixed state: For each
\ell=1,\ldots,n
, we consider the following. If
P_{\ell}=I,X,Y
, we choose
\sigma_{\ell}
from the uniform distribution over
\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert
. If
P_{\ell}=Z
, we choose
\sigma_{\ell}
from the uniform distribution over
\lvert+\rangle\!\langle+\rvert,\lvert-\rangle\!\langle-\rvert
.

•

\rho
is a locally rotated
(I\pm 0.9P)/2^{n}
: The state
(I\pm 0.9P)/2^{n}
is equal to the uniform mixture of the maximally mixed state and the state
(I\pm P)/2^{n}
. With probability
0.1
, we prepare
\rho
the same way as the maximally mixed state given above. With probability
0.9
, we prepare
\rho
as
(I\pm P)/2^{n}
.
For each
\ell=1,\ldots,n
, we consider the following. If
P_{\ell}=I
, we choose a
\sigma_{\ell}
to be a uniform distribution over
\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert,\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert
.
If
P_{\ell}\neq I
, we choose
\sigma_{\ell}
to be one of the eigenstates of
P_{\ell}
.
When
P_{\ell}
is not the last non-identity Pauli operator, we choose
\sigma_{\ell}
uniformly at random.
When
P_{\ell}
is the last non-identity Pauli operator, we choose
\sigma_{\ell}
deterministically based on the choice of
\sigma_{\ell^{\prime}}
for
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
where
P_{\ell^{\prime}}
is not an identity. The determinisitic choice satisfies a parity constraint given by the state
(I\pm P)/2^{n}
.

Other choices of the classical probability distribution that give rise to the same state would yield exactly the same result but the analysis could be slightly more complex.

L.5
Upper bound for noisy quantum-enhanced experiments

We provide a sample complexity upper bound for quantum-enhanced experiments using the noisy quantum device that we partially learned.
The quantum-enhanced experiment we implement loads in two copies of the physical system
\rho
and performs an entangled measurement across the two copies.

L.5.a
Detailed procedure

In the noisy quantum-enhanced experiments, we utilize the noisy quantum device and repeat the following for
N_{\mathrm{Q}}/2
times.

1.

Prepare the initial product state followed by a layer of single-qubit gates
g_{i,0}
for all qubit
i=1,\ldots,2n
.

2.

Load the physical system
\rho
into the left hand side of the
n\times 2
grid.

3.

Apply the entangling layer
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell=1,s}\otimes\ldots\otimes{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell=n,s}
, which is approximately equal to applying SWAP gates between left and right side of the
n\times 2
grid from Eq. (
461
).

4.

Load the physical system
\rho
into the left hand side of the
n\times 2
grid again.

5.

Apply the entangling layer
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell=1,e}\otimes\ldots\otimes{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell=n,e}
, which is approximately equal to rotating the pairs of qubits into a Bell basis from Eq. (
462
).

6.

Apply a layer of single-qubit gates
g_{i,6}
for all qubit
i=1,\ldots,2n
. Then measure using the unknown product measurement.

7.

Store the measurement outcome as
b_{t,i},\forall i=1,\ldots,2n
for the
t
-th experiment.

After the
N_{\mathrm{Q}}/2
experiments using
N_{\mathrm{Q}}
copies of the physical system
\rho
, when we are given a Pauli observable
P\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}
, we compute the following,

\Xi\equiv\frac{2}{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}\sum_{t=1}^{N_{\mathrm{Q}}/2}\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}\bra{\beta(b_{t,2\ell-1},b_{t,2\ell})}P_{\ell}\otimes P_{\ell}\ket{\beta(b_{t,2\ell-1},b_{t,2\ell})},

(468)

where
\ket{\beta(x,y)}
is the Bell state,

\ket{\beta(x,y)}=\left(\frac{\ket{0,y}+(-1)^{x}\ket{1,1-y}}{\sqrt{2}}\right).

(469)

To understand what is happening, consider
U_{i}=I,\forall i
.
When all the operations are perfect, the quantum-enhanced experiment is equivalent to storing two copies of
\rho
in the
2n
qubits and measuring every pair of corresponding qubits in the Bell basis.
The Bell basis simultaneously diagonalizes
P\otimes P
for all
P\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}
.
Hence, we can simultaneously predict
\Tr((P\otimes P)(\rho\otimes\rho))=\Tr(P\rho)^{2}
for all the Pauli observables.

L.5.b
Noise analysis

We analyze how noise affects the quantum-enhanced experiments.
Suppose the first sample of
\rho
is

(U_{1}\sigma^{A}_{1}U_{1}^{-1})\otimes\ldots\otimes(U_{2n-1}\sigma^{A}_{n}U_{2n-1}^{-1}),

(470)

and the second sample of
\rho
is

(U_{1}\sigma^{B}_{1}U_{1}^{-1})\otimes\ldots\otimes(U_{2n-1}\sigma^{B}_{n}U_{2n-1}^{-1}).

(471)

Let us focus on a pair of qubits
2\ell-1,2\ell
for
\ell=1,\ldots,n
.
From Eq. (
458
), after the first two steps, the pair of qubits is in a state
\rho^{(2\ell-1,2\ell),a}
with

\left\lVert\rho^{(2\ell-1,2\ell),a}-(U_{2\ell-1}\sigma^{A}_{\ell}U_{2\ell-1}^{-1})\otimes(U_{2\ell}\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert U_{2\ell}^{-1})\right\rVert_{1}\leq\epsilon.

(472)

After the third and fourth step, the pair of qubits is now in a state
\rho^{(2\ell-1,2\ell),b}
with

\left\lVert\rho^{(2\ell-1,2\ell),b}-(U_{2\ell-1}\sigma^{B}_{\ell}U_{2\ell-1}^{-1})\otimes(U_{2\ell}\sigma^{A}_{\ell}U_{2\ell}^{-1})\right\rVert_{1}\leq 2\epsilon

(473)

using Eq. (
461
).
After the fifth step, the two-qubit state is now
\rho^{(2\ell-1,2\ell),c}
.
From Eq. (
462
), we have

\left\lVert\rho^{(2\ell-1,2\ell),c}-(U_{2\ell-1}\otimes U_{2\ell})\mathrm{BELL}(\sigma^{B}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{A}_{\ell})\mathrm{BELL}^{\dagger}(U_{2\ell-1}^{-1}\otimes U_{2\ell}^{-1})\right\rVert_{1}\leq 3\epsilon.

(474)

In the sixth and seventh step, we measure the two-qubit state
\rho^{(2\ell-1,2\ell),c}
with a two-qubit product POVM. The two-qubit product POVM is given by

\{M_{0}^{(2\ell-1,6,6)}\otimes M_{0}^{(2\ell,6,6)},M_{0}^{(2\ell-1,6,6)}\otimes M_{1}^{(2\ell,6,6)},M_{1}^{(2\ell-1,6,6)}\otimes M_{0}^{(2\ell,6,6)},M_{1}^{(2\ell-1,6,6)}\otimes M_{1}^{(2\ell,6,6)}\},

(475)

where the approximation error is given in Eq. (
459
).

We now combine with the classical post-processing in Eq. (
468
).
Given a Pauli observable
P\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}
.
For each experiment, we can show that we are measuring the two-qubit state
\rho^{(2\ell-1,2\ell),c}
with an observable

O^{(2\ell-1,2\ell)}\equiv\sum_{x,y\in\{0,1\}}\bra{\beta(x,y)}P_{\ell}\otimes P_{\ell}\ket{\beta(x,y)}M_{x}^{(2\ell-1,6,6)}\otimes M_{y}^{(2\ell,6,6)}.

(476)

The observable
O^{(2\ell-1,2\ell)}
differs from the ideal observable

\sum_{x,y\in\{0,1\}}\bra{\beta(x,y)}P_{\ell}\otimes P_{\ell}\ket{\beta(x,y)}(U_{2\ell-1}\lvert x\rangle\!\langle x\rvert U_{2\ell-1}^{-1})\otimes(U_{2\ell}\lvert y\rangle\!\langle y\rvert U_{2\ell}^{-1})

(477)

by at most
\epsilon
error in
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\infty}
.
Using the triangle inequality, we have

\left|\Tr\left(O^{(2\ell-1,2\ell)}\rho^{(2\ell-1,2\ell),c}\right)-\sum_{x,y}\bra{\beta(x,y)}P_{\ell}\otimes P_{\ell}\ket{\beta(x,y)}\bra{\beta(x,y)}(\sigma^{B}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{A}_{\ell})\ket{\beta(x,y)}\right|\leq 4\epsilon.

(478)

Because the Bell basis simultaneously diagonalizes
\{I\otimes I,X\otimes X,Y\otimes Y,Z\otimes Z\}
, we have

\sum_{x,y}\bra{\beta(x,y)}P_{\ell}\otimes P_{\ell}\ket{\beta(x,y)}\bra{\beta(x,y)}(\sigma^{B}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{A}_{\ell})\ket{\beta(x,y)}=\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{B}_{\ell})\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{A}_{\ell}).

(479)

Recall that there exists a CPTP map
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell}
such that
\rho^{(2\ell-1,2\ell),c}={{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell}(\sigma^{A}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{B}_{\ell})
. Hence, we have

\left|\Tr\left(O^{(2\ell-1,2\ell)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell}(\sigma^{A}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{B}_{\ell})\right)-\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{A}_{\ell})\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{B}_{\ell})\right|\leq 4\epsilon.

(480)

Furthermore, when
P_{\ell}=I
, we have

\Tr\left(O^{(2\ell-1,2\ell)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell}(\sigma^{A}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{B}_{\ell})\right)=\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{A}_{\ell})\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{B}_{\ell})=1.

(481)

The two characterizations above will be crucial for the sample complexity analysis.

L.5.c
Sample complexity analysis

We use the probability distribution given in Appendix
L.4.a
to analyze the expectation value of
\Xi
in Eq. (
468
).
Combining with Eq. (
467
), we have

\displaystyle\E[\Xi]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma^{A}_{\ell}\in S,\\
\forall\ell=1,\ldots,n\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma^{B}_{\ell}\in S,\\
\forall\ell=1,\ldots,n\end{subarray}}p(\sigma^{A}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{A}_{n})p(\sigma^{B}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{B}_{n})\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}\Tr\left(O^{(2\ell-1,2\ell)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell}(\sigma^{A}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{B}_{\ell})\right).

(482)

We separate the analysis into two cases.

•

\rho
is the maximally mixed state: We only consider
\sigma^{A},\sigma^{B}
that appear with nonzero probability. For all
\ell=1,\ldots,n
, we consider whether
P_{\ell}
is equal to
I
. If
P_{\ell}\neq I
, then we have
\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{A}_{\ell})\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{B}_{\ell})=0
, hence

|\Tr\left(O^{(2\ell-1,2\ell)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell}(\sigma^{A}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{B}_{\ell})\right)|\leq 4\epsilon

(483)

from Eq. (
480
). If
P_{\ell}=I
, from Eq. (
481
), we have

\Tr\left(O^{(2\ell-1,2\ell)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell}(\sigma^{A}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{B}_{\ell})\right)=1.

(484)

Together, we have

\left|\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}\Tr\left(O^{(2\ell-1,2\ell)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell}(\sigma^{A}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{B}_{\ell})\right)\right|\leq(4\epsilon)^{\#(P_{\ell}\neq I)},

(485)

where
\#(P_{\ell}\neq I)
is the number of
P_{\ell},\forall\ell=1,\ldots,n
that is not equal to identity
I
. Therefore we conclude that

|\E[\Xi]|\leq(4\epsilon)^{\#(P_{\ell}\neq I)}.

(486)

•

\rho
is a locally rotated
(I\pm 0.9P)/2^{n}
: There is a probability of
0.19
such that at least one of
\sigma^{A},\sigma^{B}
is sampled according to the distribution for the maximally mixed state. The same analysis as the case for maximally mixed state shows that we have

\left|\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}\Tr\left(O^{(2\ell-1,2\ell)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell}(\sigma^{A}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{B}_{\ell})\right)\right|\leq(4\epsilon)^{\#(P_{\ell}\neq I)}.

(487)

For a probability of
0.81
, we have both
\sigma^{A}
and
\sigma^{B}
are sampled according to the probability distribution for
(I\pm P)/2^{n}
defined in Appendix
L.4.a
.
We focus on
\sigma^{A}
and
\sigma^{B}
that occur with non-zero probability.
We consider all
\ell=1,\ldots,n
. We again separate into two cases:
P_{\ell}\neq I
and
P_{\ell}=I
.

–

If
P_{\ell}\neq I
, then we have
\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{A}_{\ell})\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{B}_{\ell})\in\{1,-1\}
. If
\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{A}_{\ell})\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{B}_{\ell})=1
, then

\Tr\left(O^{(2\ell-1,2\ell)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell}(\sigma^{A}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{B}_{\ell})\right)\geq 1-4\epsilon

(488)

from Eq. (
480
).
In contrast, if
\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{A}_{\ell})\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{B}_{\ell})=-1
, then

\Tr\left(O^{(2\ell-1,2\ell)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell}(\sigma^{A}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{B}_{\ell})\right)\leq-1+4\epsilon.

(489)

–

If
P_{\ell}=I
, from Eq. (
481
), we have

\Tr\left(O^{(2\ell-1,2\ell)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell}(\sigma^{A}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{B}_{\ell})\right)=1.

(490)

Furthermore, the parity constraint in the probability distribution over
\sigma^{A},\sigma^{B}
given in Appendix
L.4.a
shows that

\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{A}_{\ell})\Tr(P_{\ell}\sigma^{B}_{\ell})=1.

(491)

Hence, we have

\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}\Tr\left(O^{(2\ell-1,2\ell)}{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell}(\sigma^{A}_{\ell}\otimes\sigma^{B}_{\ell})\right)\geq(1-4\epsilon)^{\#(P_{\ell}\neq I)}.

(492)

Combining with Eq. (
487
), we can conclude that

\E[\Xi]\geq 0.81(1-4\epsilon)^{\#(P_{\ell}\neq I)}-0.19(4\epsilon)^{\#(P_{\ell}\neq I)}.

(493)

Recall from Eq. (
468
),
\Xi
is the average over
N_{\mathrm{Q}}/2
independent random variables bounded between
[-1,1]
. Hence, by Hoeffding’s inequality, we need

N_{\mathrm{Q}}=\mathcal{O}(\log(1/\delta)/\tilde{\epsilon}^{2})

(494)

to estimate
\E[\Xi]
to
\tilde{\epsilon}
error with probability at least
1-\delta
.
In order to distinguish between
\rho
being the maximally mixed state or not, we need to estimate
\E[\Xi]
to an error of at most

0.81(1-4\epsilon)^{\#(P_{\ell}\neq I)}-0.19(4\epsilon)^{\#(P_{\ell}\neq I)}-(4\epsilon)^{\#(P_{\ell}\neq I)}.

(495)

For
\epsilon
less than a constant and
n
sufficiently large, the above function is minimized at
\#(P_{\ell}\neq I)=n
.
In order to predict accurately with a probability at least
0.99
, the sample complexity for the noisy quantum-enhanced experiment is

N_{\mathrm{Q}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{(1-4\epsilon)^{2n}}\right).

(496)

This concludes the proof for the first part of Theorem
L.3
.

L.6
Lower bound for noiseless conventional experiments

We give a sample complexity lower bound for conventional experiments based on adaptive POVM measurements.
We do not assume the presence of any noise in conventional experiments.
The proof uses techniques proposed in
huang2021information
;
huang2021quantum
;
chen2021exponential
.
In particular, the proof is closely related to one of the proofs in
huang2021quantum
up to minor changes. We present a concise proof here for completeness.

A learning algorithm using noiseless conventional experiments is a rooted tree.
At every node, we perform a POVM on
\rho
.
Based on the POVM outcome, we move to a child node of the node.
Because a rank-
1
POVM
\{w_{b}\lvert\psi_{b}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{b}\rvert\}_{b}
is always at least as powerful as general POVM
huang2021information
;
chen2021exponential
, we will only consider rank-
1
POVMs.
After
N_{\mathcal{C}}
experiments, we arrive at a leaf node of the tree at depth
N_{\mathcal{C}}
.
Depending on the unknown physical state
\rho
, the probability distribution over the leaf nodes will be different. We write the probability distribution as

p_{\rho}(\ell)=\prod_{t=1}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}w_{t}\bra{\psi_{t}}\rho\ket{\psi_{t}},\forall\ell:\mbox{leaf nodes},

(497)

where
w_{1}\lvert\psi_{1}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{1}\rvert,\ldots,w_{N_{\mathrm{C}}}\lvert\psi_{\mathrm{C}}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{\mathrm{C}}\rvert
are the POVM elements associated to the outcomes of the
N_{\mathrm{C}}
measurements that ends up at the leaf node
\ell
.

After the experiments, we obtain a Pauli observable
P\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}\setminus\{I^{\otimes n}\}
and the assocaited observable
O=\left(\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{n}U_{2\ell-1}\right)P\left(\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{n}U_{2\ell-1}^{-1}\right).

Suppose we can use the conventional experiments to classify between maximally mixed state

\rho_{\mathrm{mm}}=I/2^{n}

(498)

and the alternative states

\rho_{S,P}=\left(\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{n}U_{2\ell-1}\right)\left(\frac{I+S0.9P}{2^{n}}\right)\left(\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{n}U_{2\ell-1}^{-1}\right)

(499)

under the knowledge of
P
and
U_{i},\forall i
, where
S\in\{\pm 1\},P\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}\setminus\{I^{\otimes n}\}
.
Then the average total variation distance between the leaf node distribution of the maximally mixed state and the alternative states
\rho_{s,P}
must be greater than a constant,

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}\end{subarray}\setminus\{I^{\otimes n}\}}\left[\,\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell:\mathrm{leaf}}\left|p_{\ell}(\rho_{\mathrm{mm}})-\E_{S\in\{\pm 1\}}p_{\ell}\left(\rho_{S,P}\right)\right|\,\right]=\Omega(1).

(500)

The expectation over
S\in\{\pm 1\}
is in the inside because the knowledge of
S
is not revealed.
On the other hand, the expectation over
P
is on the outside because the knowledge of
P
is revealed.

We now upper bound the average total variation distance with
N_{\mathcal{C}}
.

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}\setminus\{I^{\otimes n}\}\end{subarray}}\left[\,\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell:\mathrm{leaf}}\left|p_{\ell}(\rho_{\mathrm{mm}})-\E_{S\in\{\pm 1\}}p_{\ell}\left(\rho_{S,P}\right)\right|\,\right]

(501)

\displaystyle=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}\setminus\{I^{\otimes n}\}\end{subarray}}\left[\,\sum_{\ell:\mathrm{leaf}}\max\left(0,\,\,p_{\ell}(\rho_{\mathrm{mm}})-\E_{S\in\{\pm 1\}}p_{\ell}\left(\rho_{S,P}\right)\right)\,\right]

(502)
